# Daily Summary: 2025-04-18

### Subitizing-Inspired_Large_Language_Models_for_Floorplanning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12076v1)
- **Authors**: Shao-Chien Lu, Chen-Chen Yeh, Hui-Lin Cho, Yu-Cheng Lin, Rung-Bin Lin
- **Abstract**: We present a novel approach to solving the floorplanning problem by leveraging fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs). Inspired by subitizing--the human ability to instantly and accurately count small numbers of items at a glance--we hypothesize that LLMs can similarly address floorplanning challenges swiftly and accurately. We propose an efficient representation of the floorplanning problem and introduce a method for generating high-quality datasets tailored for model fine-tuning. We fine-tune LLMs on datasets with a specified number of modules to test whether LLMs can emulate the human ability to quickly count and arrange items. Our experimental results demonstrate that fine-tuned LLMs, particularly GPT4o-mini, achieve high success and optimal rates while attaining relatively low average dead space. These findings underscore the potential of LLMs as promising solutions for complex optimization tasks in VLSI design.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper on subitizing-inspired large language models for floorplanning:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to VLSI floorplanning by leveraging fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs).  Inspired by the human cognitive ability of subitizing, the authors hypothesize that LLMs can quickly and accurately arrange modules on a chip. They define a concise representation of the floorplanning problem, create a dataset generation method based on recursive slicing and tree encoding, and fine-tune various LLMs (including GPT40-mini, LLaMA variants, Mistral, and Phi-4) on datasets with varying module counts (16 and 24).  The results demonstrate that fine-tuned LLMs, especially GPT40-mini, achieve high success rates in generating legal slicing trees and can achieve low average dead space compared to other models. The authors conclude that LLMs offer a promising avenue for tackling complex optimization problems in VLSI design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of LLMs to VLSI floorplanning is relatively novel, especially with the subitizing inspiration. The paper makes a good first step in exploring this intersection. The dataset generation approach using recursive slicing and tree encoding seems unique and well-suited for LLM input/output. However, using LLMs as a "co-pilot" for conventional floorplanners isn't entirely a groundbreaking idea; the novelty lies primarily in the specific implementation and the subitizing analogy. The comparison to human number recognition is a good rhetorical hook, but its direct impact on the LLM approach itself seems limited.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate.  The floorplanning problem is critical in VLSI design, and any approach that can improve efficiency and reduce dead space is valuable. The results showing that fine-tuned LLMs can generate legal slicing trees and, in some cases, optimal floorplans is encouraging. However, the experiments are limited in scope (small module counts, only considering dead space) and the results aren't directly comparable to well established floorplanning algorithms. Demonstrating any practical improvement compared to SA or ILP is entirely missing.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Methodology:** The paper clearly defines the floorplanning problem, the chosen slicing tree representation, and the data generation/fine-tuning process.
    *   **Interesting Analogy:** The subitizing inspiration provides a compelling narrative.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:**  The experiments with various LLMs provide some insights into their performance on this task, although the dataset is limited to artificial data and needs to be expanded to be meaningful.
    *   **Thorough reporting on Hyperparameters** All relevant hyperparameters are provided making the work reproducible.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments only focus on minimizing dead space and use relatively small module counts. Real-world floorplanning involves many more constraints (wire length, thermal considerations, power consumption, pin placement, routing congestion), which are completely ignored.
    *   **Artificial Datasets:**  Using only synthetic data limits the generalizability of the results. The model is trained and tested on the same kind of artificial benchmark, making it unlikely to generalise to complex real-world circuits.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to State-of-the-Art:**  The paper fails to compare the LLM approach to established floorplanning algorithms like Simulated Annealing (SA), Integer Linear Programming (ILP), or even more recent machine learning methods. This makes it difficult to assess the true value of the LLM-based approach. The comparison to "traditional methods" in the Introduction is superficial and lacks empirical backing.
    *   **GPT40-mini limitations** - GPT40-mini is deprecated now making the work unable to be reproduced in its current form. There are potentially better LLMs out there to explore.
    *   **GPT40-mini dependence:** The results are heavily dependent on GPT40-mini and the results of different LLMs are only marginally good and not reproducible by better models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could potentially spark further research into using LLMs for VLSI design automation, especially for tasks like floorplanning, partitioning, and timing analysis. However, its influence will be limited unless future work addresses the weaknesses mentioned above, particularly by incorporating more realistic constraints and datasets and providing rigorous comparisons to existing methods.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the limited scope, reliance on artificial data, lack of comparison to established techniques, and significant unmet challenges (scalability, constraint handling, practical integration), the paper doesn't represent a major breakthrough. However, it's a reasonably well-executed initial exploration of a potentially promising avenue. The subitizing analogy and dataset generation method add some interest. Therefore:

**Score: 5**

The score is on the lower end of "moderate" because while the initial exploration is done reasonably well, the lack of demonstration or discussion of the practical significance of this method for real circuits is notable and crucial.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 5/10

### Selective Demonstration Retrieval for Improved Implicit Hate Speech Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12082v1)
- **Authors**: Yumin Kim, Hwanhee Lee
- **Abstract**: Hate speech detection is a crucial area of research in natural language processing, essential for ensuring online community safety. However, detecting implicit hate speech, where harmful intent is conveyed in subtle or indirect ways, remains a major challenge. Unlike explicit hate speech, implicit expressions often depend on context, cultural subtleties, and hidden biases, making them more challenging to identify consistently. Additionally, the interpretation of such speech is influenced by external knowledge and demographic biases, resulting in varied detection results across different language models. Furthermore, Large Language Models often show heightened sensitivity to toxic language and references to vulnerable groups, which can lead to misclassifications. This over-sensitivity results in false positives (incorrectly identifying harmless statements as hateful) and false negatives (failing to detect genuinely harmful content). Addressing these issues requires methods that not only improve detection precision but also reduce model biases and enhance robustness. To address these challenges, we propose a novel method, which utilizes in-context learning without requiring model fine-tuning. By adaptively retrieving demonstrations that focus on similar groups or those with the highest similarity scores, our approach enhances contextual comprehension. Experimental results show that our method outperforms current state-of-the-art techniques. Implementation details and code are available at TBD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of detecting implicit hate speech, which is subtle and context-dependent, making it difficult for standard language models to identify accurately. The authors propose Adaptive Retrieval-based In-context Learning (ARIIHA), a novel framework that leverages in-context learning without fine-tuning. ARIIHA adaptively retrieves demonstrations (examples) to guide the model, prioritizing those with similar target groups or high similarity scores. This dynamic retrieval strategy is modulated by thresholds derived from similarity scores and the model's reliance on shortcut cues. Experimental results on the Implicit Hate Corpus (IHC) dataset show that ARIIHA outperforms existing state-of-the-art techniques, demonstrating enhanced detection accuracy and reduced over-sensitivity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach to in-context learning for hate speech detection. The adaptive retrieval mechanism, combining target group prioritization with similarity scores and shortcut cue detection, is a significant contribution. The idea of dynamically switching between retrieval strategies based on thresholds is a strong point, showing adaptability to the specific input. The paper is also the first to propose a retrieval-based in-context learning framework for implicit hate speech.

*   **Significance:** Implicit hate speech detection is a critical area of research. ARIIHA's ability to improve detection accuracy while reducing over-sensitivity is significant. Over-sensitivity, which leads to false positives, is a common problem in hate speech detection, and the paper's approach directly tackles this issue. The demonstration of the method's effectiveness on a challenging dataset like IHC further strengthens its significance. The ablations performed clearly show the impact of the proposed methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The adaptive retrieval mechanism is well-designed and theoretically sound.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate clear improvements over existing methods.
    *   The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   The case study provides further explanation on how ARIIHA works in certain situations.
    *   The approach does not require fine-tuning, making it more accessible and scalable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the thresholds seem crucial to the approach, it's unclear how robust these thresholds are. They are optimized on the dev set, but the generalization of these thresholds to unseen datasets isn't discussed in depth.
    *   The target group prediction stage introduces a potential point of failure. If the target group is incorrectly predicted, the subsequent retrieval process will be compromised. More analysis on the impact of incorrect target group predictions would be valuable.
    *   The paper references Qwen2.5-7B, but its reliance on the model for evaluation is concerning since Qwen2 is a relatively new LLM compared to, for example, Llama2 or Mistral.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in hate speech detection and in-context learning. The adaptive retrieval mechanism could be adopted and extended in other tasks where context and nuance are crucial. Furthermore, its method can be extended in the future to consider multimodal hate speech data, further increasing the significance of the method.

**Justification for Score:**

ARIIHA makes a strong contribution to the field of implicit hate speech detection. The adaptive retrieval method is novel and effective, addressing a critical challenge in the area. The weaknesses are primarily related to the generalizability of the thresholds and the potential dependence on the accurate target group prediction. Overall, the method has significant potential to improve hate speech detection systems.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Reasoning-Based AI for Startup Evaluation (R.A.I.S.E.): A Memory-Augmented, Multi-Step Decision Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12090v1)
- **Authors**: Jack Preuveneers, Joseph Ternasky, Fuat Alican, Yigit Ihlamur
- **Abstract**: We present a novel framework that bridges the gap between the interpretability of decision trees and the advanced reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to predict startup success. Our approach leverages chain-of-thought prompting to generate detailed reasoning logs, which are subsequently distilled into structured, human-understandable logical rules. The pipeline integrates multiple enhancements - efficient data ingestion, a two-step refinement process, ensemble candidate sampling, simulated reinforcement learning scoring, and persistent memory - to ensure both stable decision-making and transparent output. Experimental evaluations on curated startup datasets demonstrate that our combined pipeline improves precision by 54% from 0.225 to 0.346 and accuracy by 50% from 0.46 to 0.70 compared to a standalone OpenAI o3 model. Notably, our model achieves over 2x the precision of a random classifier (16%). By combining state-of-the-art AI reasoning with explicit rule-based explanations, our method not only augments traditional decision-making processes but also facilitates expert intervention and continuous policy refinement. This work lays the foundation for the implementation of interpretable LLM-powered decision frameworks in high-stakes investment environments and other domains that require transparent and data-driven insights.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces R.A.I.S.E., a novel framework for startup evaluation that combines the interpretability of decision trees with the advanced reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The system leverages chain-of-thought prompting to generate detailed reasoning logs, distills them into human-understandable logical rules, and integrates multiple enhancements, including efficient data ingestion, a two-step refinement process, ensemble candidate sampling, simulated reinforcement learning scoring, and persistent memory. The framework demonstrates improved precision and accuracy compared to a standalone OpenAI 03 model on curated startup datasets and aims to provide interpretable explanations and facilitate expert intervention in decision-making.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of combining LLMs with rule-based systems for interpretable decision-making is a valuable contribution. The integration of techniques like ensemble candidate sampling and simulated RL scoring to refine LLM reasoning logs and decision policies enhances the existing methodologies. While each component may not be entirely new, their combined application in this context and the systematic way they are integrated to address the specific problem of startup evaluation demonstrates novelty. The work builds upon existing research, notably GPTree, and introduces meaningful improvements to improve precision and human interpretability.

*   **Significance:** The framework is significant because it attempts to bridge the gap between the black-box nature of LLMs and the need for transparency in high-stakes investment decisions. By producing human-understandable explanations alongside predictions, it facilitates trust and expert oversight. The modular design allows for continuous refinement and policy adaptation, potentially leading to more robust and effective investment strategies. The demonstrated improvements in precision, while potentially modest in absolute terms, can have a substantial impact in the venture capital domain where identifying successful startups is critical.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Interpretability:** The explicit rule-based explanations provide transparency, enabling domain experts to understand and intervene in the decision-making process.
    *   **Modular Design:** The framework's modularity allows for iterative improvements and facilitates integration with existing decision-making processes.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a systematic evaluation of different enhancements, demonstrating their individual and combined contributions.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The framework addresses a real-world problem with significant financial implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Scope:** The evaluation is performed on a curated dataset with a fixed test set, which may not fully reflect the complexity and diversity of real-world startup data. The training and test set size (100+10+50) could be considered relatively small in the context of LLMs.
    *   **Modest Improvements:** The performance gains from individual enhancements, while consistent, are not always substantial. The significant improvements comes mainly from the combination of several models.
    *   **Dependence on LLM Quality:** The framework's performance is still reliant on the quality of the underlying LLM.
    *   **Token Usage Cost:** The significant increase in token usage and computation costs due to the multistep approach presents a limitation for real-world deployment.
    *   **Specific to the 03-mini LLM?** Does this pipeline and integration works as well as for other models as it does for the 03-mini model?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more interpretable and trustworthy AI systems for decision-making in high-stakes domains. The framework's modular design and focus on explanation can serve as a blueprint for similar applications in healthcare, finance, and other fields where transparency is essential. The systematic evaluation of different enhancements can also guide future research in this area.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

While the paper presents a novel approach with promising results, the limitations in dataset scope, model architecture, token usage cost, and magnitude of improvements necessitate a more conservative scoring.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Gauging Overprecision in LLMs: An Empirical Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12098v1)
- **Authors**: Adil Bahaj, Hamed Rahimi, Mohamed Chetouani, Mounir Ghogho
- **Abstract**: Recently, overconfidence in large language models (LLMs) has garnered considerable attention due to its fundamental importance in quantifying the trustworthiness of LLM generation. However, existing approaches prompt the \textit{black box LLMs} to produce their confidence (\textit{verbalized confidence}), which can be subject to many biases and hallucinations. Inspired by a different aspect of overconfidence in cognitive science called \textit{overprecision}, we designed a framework for its study in black box LLMs. This framework contains three main phases: 1) generation, 2) refinement and 3) evaluation. In the generation phase we prompt the LLM to generate answers to numerical questions in the form of intervals with a certain level of confidence. This confidence level is imposed in the prompt and not required for the LLM to generate as in previous approaches. We use various prompting techniques and use the same prompt multiple times to gauge the effects of randomness in the generation process. In the refinement phase, answers from the previous phase are refined to generate better answers. The LLM answers are evaluated and studied in the evaluation phase to understand its internal workings. This study allowed us to gain various insights into LLM overprecision: 1) LLMs are highly uncalibrated for numerical tasks 2) {\color{blue}there is no correlation between the length of the interval and the imposed confidence level, which can be symptomatic of a a) lack of understanding of the concept of confidence or b) inability to adjust self-confidence by following instructions}, {\color{blue}3)} LLM numerical precision differs depending on the task, scale of answer and prompting technique {\color{blue}4) Refinement of answers doesn't improve precision in most cases}. We believe this study offers new perspectives on LLM overconfidence and serves as a strong baseline for overprecision in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary**

The paper "Gauging Overprecision in LLMs: An Empirical Study" investigates overprecision, a type of overconfidence defined as unwarranted certainty in the accuracy of knowledge, in Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors design a framework consisting of three phases: (1) *Generation*, where LLMs are prompted to provide numerical answers as intervals with an imposed confidence level; (2) *Refinement*, where answers are refined through aggregation or self-refinement techniques; and (3) *Evaluation*, where the LLM's performance is assessed using cognitive science-inspired metrics like "hit rate". The study explores different prompting strategies (Vanilla and Chain-of-Thought), sampling techniques (Self-Random, Misleading), and refinement methods to understand factors influencing overprecision. Key findings include: LLMs are poorly calibrated for numerical tasks, there is a lack of correlation between the imposed confidence levels and the interval lengths, numerical precision varies with task and prompting strategy, and refinement strategies offer limited improvements, with self-refinement often degrading performance.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty in Focus:**  The paper shifts the focus of LLM overconfidence research from overestimation to overprecision, a less explored but more robust concept in cognitive science. This is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Well-Designed Framework:** The proposed framework is systematic and allows for controlled experimentation. The three-phase approach facilitates a granular analysis of LLM behavior.
    *   **Use of Cognitive Science Inspired Metrics:** The adoption of metrics like "hit rate" and Pearson correlation provides a quantitative and theoretically grounded way to assess overprecision.
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Study:** The paper includes a thorough empirical investigation, considering various datasets, models, prompting techniques, and refinement strategies. The variety of experiments adds depth to the analysis.
    *   **Emphasis on Numerical Reasoning:** By focusing on numerical reasoning the authors avoids biases present in categorical tasks like order bias and authoring bias.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Selection:** The study primarily uses GPT-3.5-turbo and GPT-4o-Mini.  While these are popular models, a wider range of models, especially open-source models and different architectures (e.g., those specialized in numerical tasks), would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Black-Box Approach:** The reliance on black-box methods, while practical, limits the ability to deeply understand the internal mechanisms driving overprecision.  White-box analysis, though more challenging, could offer valuable insights.
    *   **Ambiguity in Causes:** While the study identifies a lack of correlation between imposed confidence and interval length, it only speculates as to the underlying reasons (lack of understanding vs. inability to adjust self-confidence). This ambiguity should be addressed using model analysis.
    *   **Contradictory Findings:** The self-refinement degraded performance which contradicts findings in cognitive science and some LLM tasks. This contradiction highlights the need for a more nuanced understanding of when and how to apply self-refinement strategies in LLMs.
    *   **Limited Refinement Strategies:** The paper only investigated two refinement strategies. It would be nice if more diverse methods like external knowledge integration and iterative refinement loops were considered.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to a more nuanced understanding of LLM overconfidence, moving beyond simple overestimation.
    *   The proposed framework provides a useful tool for evaluating and comparing overprecision in different LLMs.
    *   The negative results regarding self-refinement are important, as they challenge existing assumptions and suggest that simply prompting an LLM to refine its own answers may not be effective for numerical tasks.
    *   The identification of factors like task type and answer scale impacting precision can inform the development of more targeted mitigation strategies.
    *   The results serve as a strong baseline for future overprecision studies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed study on an important aspect of LLM reliability. The strengths lie in the novel focus, systematic framework, comprehensive empirical analysis, and use of cognitive science principles. The weaknesses are primarily due to resource limitations (model selection, black-box approach) and some ambiguity in the interpretation of findings. Overall, the paper provides valuable insights and a good starting point for future studies on overprecision.

Score: 7

The paper is a significant contribution, but the limitations prevent it from reaching a higher score. Further research addressing the identified weaknesses could significantly increase its impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generalized Visual Relation Detection with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12100v1)
- **Authors**: Kaifeng Gao, Siqi Chen, Hanwang Zhang, Jun Xiao, Yueting Zhuang, Qianru Sun
- **Abstract**: Visual relation detection (VRD) aims to identify relationships (or interactions) between object pairs in an image. Although recent VRD models have achieved impressive performance, they are all restricted to pre-defined relation categories, while failing to consider the semantic ambiguity characteristic of visual relations. Unlike objects, the appearance of visual relations is always subtle and can be described by multiple predicate words from different perspectives, e.g., ``ride'' can be depicted as ``race'' and ``sit on'', from the sports and spatial position views, respectively. To this end, we propose to model visual relations as continuous embeddings, and design diffusion models to achieve generalized VRD in a conditional generative manner, termed Diff-VRD. We model the diffusion process in a latent space and generate all possible relations in the image as an embedding sequence. During the generation, the visual and text embeddings of subject-object pairs serve as conditional signals and are injected via cross-attention. After the generation, we design a subsequent matching stage to assign the relation words to subject-object pairs by considering their semantic similarities. Benefiting from the diffusion-based generative process, our Diff-VRD is able to generate visual relations beyond the pre-defined category labels of datasets. To properly evaluate this generalized VRD task, we introduce two evaluation metrics, i.e., text-to-image retrieval and SPICE PR Curve inspired by image captioning. Extensive experiments in both human-object interaction (HOI) detection and scene graph generation (SGG) benchmarks attest to the superiority and effectiveness of Diff-VRD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diff-VRD, a diffusion-based model for generalized visual relation detection (VRD). Unlike existing VRD models that are limited to pre-defined relation categories, Diff-VRD models visual relations as continuous embeddings and uses diffusion models to generate relations in a conditional generative manner. The model takes visual and text embeddings of subject-object pairs as conditional signals during a diffusion process, and uses a matching stage to assign relation words to pairs based on semantic similarity. To evaluate the generalized VRD task, the paper introduces two new evaluation metrics: text-to-image retrieval and SPICE precision-recall curve. Experiments on human-object interaction (HOI) detection and scene graph generation (SGG) benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of Diff-VRD.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach to VRD by framing it as a conditional generation problem using diffusion models. This is a significant departure from traditional discriminative classification methods used in prior work. The idea of modeling relations as continuous embeddings rather than discrete categories is also relatively novel in the VRD space, directly addressing the semantic ambiguity of visual relationships. However, diffusion models and their application to vision-language tasks (like captioning) are not entirely new, so the novelty primarily lies in *how* the diffusion model is adapted and applied to the specific problem of VRD, and in introducing new evaluation metrics. The use of CLIP embeddings is now fairly standard.

*   **Significance:** The main significance lies in addressing the limitation of existing VRD methods that are constrained by pre-defined relation categories. Diff-VRD has the potential to identify visual relations beyond the annotations of datasets, leading to a more comprehensive scene understanding. The introduction of text-to-image retrieval and SPICE PR curves as evaluation metrics is also a valuable contribution, as it enables the evaluation of reasonable but unannotated relations, a crucial aspect of generalized VRD. If successful, it can impact applications involving high-level scene understanding, human-computer interaction, and robotics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of diffusion models to address the semantic ambiguity of visual relations is well-motivated.
    *   The two new evaluation metrics are a valuable contribution to the field.
    *   Experiments on standard benchmarks show promising results, especially in T2I retrieval.
    *   The model can be applied as an enhancement module to existing VRD models.
    *   The qualitative results demonstrate the capability of Diff-VRD to predict reasonable relations beyond the pre-defined categories.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper mentions a "commonsense-based post-processing" step to filter out unreasonable predictions. The details are not clear, and such a step may introduce biases that are not properly accounted for.
    *   The results are still somewhat limited, with some performance drops compared to existing VRD models when evaluated on traditional metrics using pre-defined categories (without considering the newly generated relations).
    *   The experiments are mainly focused on retrieval metrics, and while the diversity metrics are considered, it would have been even stronger to have human evaluation/validation of the expanded diversity and relation detection accuracy.
    *   The model relies on CLIP embeddings, which might limit its ability to generalize to scenarios where CLIP performs poorly.
    *   The computational cost of the diffusion process should be analyzed, as it may be a limiting factor for real-time applications.

* **Rigorous Rationale**
The core contribution is the **innovative adaptation of diffusion models for generalized VRD**, specifically tailored to capture the subtle nuances of visual relationships by modelling them as continuous embeddings. While diffusion models are used across a wide range of tasks including VLM, the novel implementation and the new evaluation metrics justify the score.

The paper adequately addresses the limitations in the evaluation metrics using Text-to-Image Retrieval and SPICE PR curves, since the traditional evaluation metrics are primarily focused on fixed benchmarks and therefore not the optimal proxy to gauge the generated interactions.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Entropy-Guided Watermarking for LLMs: A Test-Time Framework for Robust and Traceable Text Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12108v1)
- **Authors**: Shizhan Cai, Liang Ding, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has intensified concerns about content traceability and potential misuse. Existing watermarking schemes for sampled text often face trade-offs between maintaining text quality and ensuring robust detection against various attacks. To address these issues, we propose a novel watermarking scheme that improves both detectability and text quality by introducing a cumulative watermark entropy threshold. Our approach is compatible with and generalizes existing sampling functions, enhancing adaptability. Experimental results across multiple LLMs show that our scheme significantly outperforms existing methods, achieving over 80\% improvements on widely-used datasets, e.g., MATH and GSM8K, while maintaining high detection accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel watermarking scheme for Large Language Models (LLMs) aimed at improving both detectability and text quality.  The key innovation is the introduction of a cumulative watermark entropy threshold.  Below this threshold, the output remains unwatermarked.  Above the threshold, watermarking is applied using preceding tokens as a seed to generate the key. This approach is designed to be compatible with and generalize existing sampling functions. Experimental results on various LLMs indicate significant improvements over existing methods in terms of text quality (especially on long-answer tasks) while maintaining high detection accuracy, even under paraphrase attacks. The authors also offer theoretical analysis proving the indistinguishability and provide the source code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The concept of using an entropy threshold to dynamically determine when to apply watermarking is a significant contribution.  Existing methods often apply watermarking uniformly, which can degrade the quality of already deterministic or low-entropy text.  By adaptively controlling the watermarking process based on entropy, the proposed method offers a more refined approach that strikes a better balance between detectability and text quality. Adapting the binary sampling and constructing a new mapping are also innovative.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in LLM watermarking: the trade-off between detectability and text quality.  The experimental results show substantial improvements in text quality, particularly for long-answer tasks. This is important because LLMs are increasingly used for tasks that require generating coherent and informative text. The improved robustness against paraphrase attacks also enhances the practical utility of the watermarking scheme in real-world scenarios where adversaries might try to remove the watermark. Providing the source code increases the potential impact.
* **Strengths:**
    *   The entropy threshold is an elegant and effective solution to the detectability/quality trade-off.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, covering multiple LLMs, datasets, and attack methods.
    *   The paper includes theoretical analysis to support the design choices and claims.
    *   The results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing methods, particularly in long-answer question answering scenarios.
    *   The approach appears to be easily adaptable to existing sampling functions.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the evaluation covers several models, further testing on larger and more recent LLMs would strengthen the results.
    *   The performance gains appear more pronounced on long-answer tasks. A more detailed analysis of performance on shorter text generation tasks would be useful.
    * The detailed explanation of mapping functions might be too complex to read.
    *   While robustness against paraphrase attacks is addressed, exploring more advanced attack methods (e.g., those using LLMs themselves to rephrase the text) could provide a more stringent evaluation.
    * The contribution is built on top of pre-existing watermarking methods.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future watermarking schemes for LLMs. The entropy threshold approach could become a standard technique for improving text quality while maintaining detectability. The code release will accelerate the adoption of the approach by other researchers and practitioners.

* **Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-validated watermarking scheme that makes a significant contribution to the field.  The entropy threshold is a clever idea that addresses a crucial limitation of existing methods. The experimental results are compelling, and the theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation for the approach.  While there are some limitations (scope of models evaluated, more advanced attacks) and there are many pre-existing works in the field, the strengths significantly outweigh the weaknesses. I assign a score of 8, indicating a valuable and impactful contribution that is likely to influence future research and development in LLM watermarking.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Diffusion-Based Framework for Terrain-Aware Remote Sensing Image Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12112v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Yu, Mohd Yamani Inda Idris, Pei Wang
- **Abstract**: Remote sensing imagery is essential for environmental monitoring, agricultural management, and disaster response. However, data loss due to cloud cover, sensor failures, or incomplete acquisition-especially in high-resolution and high-frequency tasks-severely limits satellite imagery's effectiveness. Traditional interpolation methods struggle with large missing areas and complex structures. Remote sensing imagery consists of multiple bands, each with distinct meanings, and ensuring consistency across bands is critical to avoid anomalies in the combined images. This paper proposes SatelliteMaker, a diffusion-based method that reconstructs missing data across varying levels of data loss while maintaining spatial, spectral, and temporal consistency. We also propose Digital Elevation Model (DEM) as a conditioning input and use tailored prompts to generate realistic images, making diffusion models applicable to quantitative remote sensing tasks. Additionally, we propose a VGG-Adapter module based on Distribution Loss, which reduces distribution discrepancy and ensures style consistency. Extensive experiments show that SatelliteMaker achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SatelliteMaker, a diffusion-based framework for reconstructing missing data in remote sensing images, addressing challenges such as cloud occlusion, sensor failures, and data discrepancy. SatelliteMaker integrates a diffusion model with Digital Elevation Models (DEMs) as conditional inputs, tailored prompts for realistic image generation, and a VGG-Adapter module with a distribution loss to reduce color and style inconsistencies. The method aims to maintain spatial, spectral, and temporal consistency during reconstruction. Experiments on Landsat-8 and EarthNet2021 datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in spatial and temporal data completion.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a practical problem:** The paper tackles a significant issue in remote sensing – data loss due to cloud cover or sensor malfunction – which severely limits the usability of satellite imagery.
    *   **Novel combination of techniques:** The use of diffusion models for remote sensing image reconstruction is promising and relatively underexplored. The integration of DEMs for conditional guidance and the VGG-Adapter for style consistency represents a novel and potentially effective approach.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experiments on two different datasets (Landsat-8 and EarthNet2021) and demonstrates superior performance compared to several existing methods, including inpainting-based methods and other diffusion models.
    *   **Focus on consistency:** The explicit focus on maintaining spatial, spectral, and temporal consistency is crucial for remote sensing applications, differentiating the approach from generic image inpainting. The results validate that the presented approach yields this consistency well.
    *   **Good Ablation Study:** The ablation study convincingly demonstrates the effectiveness of the VGG-Adapter module in improving perceptual quality and reducing distribution shifts. Also, it shows resilience against rising missing data ratios.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:** While the combination of techniques is novel, the individual components (diffusion models, DEM conditioning, VGG-Adapter) are well-established.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework involves several components, potentially increasing the complexity of implementation and optimization.
    *   **Dataset Dependence:** The performance of SatelliteMaker may be sensitive to the specific characteristics of the training datasets.
    *   **Qualitative Evaluation:** The paper contains mostly quantitative evaluations. While these are relevant, adding more robust qualitative comparisons would be highly beneficial.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Prompt Engineering:** The prompt engineering aspect, while mentioned, isn't deeply explored, leaving room for improvement.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly improve the quality and usability of remote sensing data.
    *   The framework can be extended to various remote sensing applications such as environmental monitoring, disaster response, and climate change studies.
    *   The incorporation of DEMs as conditional inputs could inspire further research on integrating external geospatial information into deep learning models.
    *   The work could stimulate more research applying recent advances in generative modeling, like diffusion models, for remote sensing tasks.

*   **Overall Impression:**

    The paper provides a well-executed and clearly presented approach to an important problem in remote sensing. While the core building blocks are not entirely new, the novel integration of diffusion models with DEM conditioning and a VGG-Adapter results in a significant performance improvement. The comprehensive experiments and ablation study strengthen the claims.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper earns a score of 7 due to its practical significance, novel combination of methods, and solid experimental results. The main limitations are the relative maturity of the individual components and the limited exploration of prompt engineering. While the approach demonstrates performance gains, the degree of innovation doesn't reach the level that would warrant a higher score. The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field, improving the usability of satellite imagery. It will also likely influence future research in applying generative models for remote sensing.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Clarifying Ambiguities: on the Role of Ambiguity Types in Prompting Methods for Clarification Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12113v1)
- **Authors**: Anfu Tang, Laure Soulier, Vincent Guigue
- **Abstract**: In information retrieval (IR), providing appropriate clarifications to better understand users' information needs is crucial for building a proactive search-oriented dialogue system. Due to the strong in-context learning ability of large language models (LLMs), recent studies investigate prompting methods to generate clarifications using few-shot or Chain of Thought (CoT) prompts. However, vanilla CoT prompting does not distinguish the characteristics of different information needs, making it difficult to understand how LLMs resolve ambiguities in user queries. In this work, we focus on the concept of ambiguity for clarification, seeking to model and integrate ambiguities in the clarification process. To this end, we comprehensively study the impact of prompting schemes based on reasoning and ambiguity for clarification. The idea is to enhance the reasoning abilities of LLMs by limiting CoT to predict first ambiguity types that can be interpreted as instructions to clarify, then correspondingly generate clarifications. We name this new prompting scheme Ambiguity Type-Chain of Thought (AT-CoT). Experiments are conducted on various datasets containing human-annotated clarifying questions to compare AT-CoT with multiple baselines. We also perform user simulations to implicitly measure the quality of generated clarifications under various IR scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the role of ambiguity types (ATs) in improving clarification generation in information retrieval (IR) systems. It argues that current approaches using Chain of Thought (CoT) prompting in large language models (LLMs) don't explicitly address the nature of ambiguities in user queries. To address this, the authors propose AMBIGUITY TYPE-CHAIN OF THOUGHT (AT-CoT), a prompting scheme that first prompts the LLM to predict ambiguity types based on a defined taxonomy and then generates clarifying questions (CQs) accordingly. The paper defines an action-based AT taxonomy (Semantic, Generalize, Specify), conducts experiments on clarification generation and IR tasks using datasets like Qulac, ClariQ, and TREC, and validates their approach via user simulation. Results suggest that AT-CoT outperforms baseline methods like standard prompting, AT-standard prompting, and vanilla CoT prompting, showing benefits in both intrinsic clarification generation and extrinsic IR performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in explicitly integrating ambiguity types into the LLM's reasoning process for clarification generation. While CoT prompting is already explored in the field, prompting to explicitly identify and leverage *specific* ambiguity types as a first step represents a tangible advance. The creation of a distilled, action-based ambiguity type taxonomy contributes, though its simplicity might also be viewed as a limitation.  The dual-function of the taxonomy (helping LLMs understand ambiguity causes and guiding LLM actions) is a well-articulated and valuable point. However, the extent to which this taxonomy is truly "new" needs to be considered as it derives from existing taxonomies. The novelty partially hinges on the *combination* of ideas rather than the invention of entirely novel concepts.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a critical issue in conversational IR: how to better understand and resolve ambiguities in user queries to enhance search performance. By enhancing LLM reasoning to account for underlying ambiguities the AT-CoT approach demonstrates potential for improving IR systems. The extrinsic IR evaluation with user simulation strengthens the claims. The analysis of which ambiguity types are most prevalent and how they impact performance provides valuable insights.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined research questions and clear methodology.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on multiple datasets and tasks (clarification generation, IR).
    *   Use of user simulation to evaluate IR performance provides a practical context.
    *   In-depth analysis of the results, including the impact of different ambiguity levels and the distribution of predicted ambiguity types.
    *   Robust methodology that supports the claims.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The AT taxonomy, while useful, is relatively simple (Semantic, Generalize, Specify). More complex, hierarchical taxonomies might provide further improvements, although the authors provide rationale for its conciseness.
    *   The "user simulation" relies on LLMs simulating user behavior, which can introduce biases.
    *   The paper acknowledges a limitation about the document collection not always being able to demonstrate the improvements by CG because relevant information is not available.
    *   There is not sufficient clarity around what LLM the AT-COT model is built upon.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of conversational IR by explicitly modeling and integrating ambiguity types into LLM-based clarification generation. The AT-CoT approach demonstrates promising results in both intrinsic and extrinsic evaluations, suggesting that it can effectively improve the quality of clarifications and enhance search performance. The robust methodology, comprehensive experiments, and in-depth analysis support the claims. Despite the relatively simple AT taxonomy and the limitations of user simulation, the paper's novelty in combining ambiguity awareness with CoT prompting, coupled with its potential for practical application, makes it a meaningful contribution. The potential limitations acknowledged in the conclusion, highlight potential improvements for future work. However, the results show consistent improvements across the board, as well as supporting the robustness of the methodology, and the conclusion is based on the results presented in the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Anti-Aesthetics: Protecting Facial Privacy against Customized Text-to-Image Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12129v1)
- **Authors**: Songping Wang, Yueming Lyu, Shiqi Liu, Ning Li, Tong Tong, Hao Sun, Caifeng Shan
- **Abstract**: The rise of customized diffusion models has spurred a boom in personalized visual content creation, but also poses risks of malicious misuse, severely threatening personal privacy and copyright protection. Some studies show that the aesthetic properties of images are highly positively correlated with human perception of image quality. Inspired by this, we approach the problem from a novel and intriguing aesthetic perspective to degrade the generation quality of maliciously customized models, thereby achieving better protection of facial identity. Specifically, we propose a Hierarchical Anti-Aesthetic (HAA) framework to fully explore aesthetic cues, which consists of two key branches: 1) Global Anti-Aesthetics: By establishing a global anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and a global anti-aesthetic loss, it can degrade the overall aesthetics of the generated content; 2) Local Anti-Aesthetics: A local anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and a local anti-aesthetic loss are designed to guide adversarial perturbations to disrupt local facial identity. By seamlessly integrating both branches, our HAA effectively achieves the goal of anti-aesthetics from a global to a local level during customized generation. Extensive experiments show that HAA outperforms existing SOTA methods largely in identity removal, providing a powerful tool for protecting facial privacy and copyright.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the privacy and copyright risks associated with personalized text-to-image synthesis using diffusion models.  It proposes a Hierarchical Anti-Aesthetic (HAA) framework designed to degrade the generation quality of maliciously customized models, thereby protecting facial privacy. HAA consists of two branches: (1) Global Anti-Aesthetics, which degrades the overall aesthetic properties of the generated content, and (2) Local Anti-Aesthetics, which disrupts local facial identity by guiding adversarial perturbations. The framework aims to degrade image quality from a global and local perspective, making it harder to create recognizable and malicious personalized content.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their method through extensive experiments and comparisons with existing state-of-the-art privacy protection techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces an interesting and novel perspective by using anti-aesthetics as a defense mechanism against malicious use of customized diffusion models.  While adversarial attacks are not new in the general sense, applying them specifically through the lens of manipulating aesthetic properties for privacy protection is a distinct contribution. The hierarchical approach, combining global and local anti-aesthetic strategies, adds another layer of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is considerable.  As personalized content generation becomes more widespread, protecting individual privacy and copyright is crucial.  The paper's approach to degrading image quality strategically, rather than simply adding noise, could be more effective in preventing malicious use while minimizing the impact on legitimate applications. The empirical validation shows HAA significantly outperforms existing methods in removing facial identity.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Innovative Approach:** Using aesthetics as a defense strategy is a compelling idea. It leverages the understanding of human perception to disrupt malicious content generation.
    *   **Hierarchical Design:** The separation of global and local anti-aesthetic components allows for a more nuanced and targeted attack on the customized diffusion models.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation with multiple datasets, diffusion models, and comparison methods, demonstrating the effectiveness of HAA.
    *   **Robustness Testing:** The inclusion of black-box testing with different DM versions and robustness tests against various image perturbations strengthens the practical value of the method.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Aesthetic Metrics:** The success of the method depends heavily on the effectiveness of the aesthetic reward models (RMg and RM1). These models are trained on specific datasets, and their performance might degrade if applied to images with different styles or content.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The iterative training process, involving both adversarial noise generation and surrogate model optimization, could be computationally expensive. The paper does not address its efficiency explicitly.
    *   **Potential for Circumvention:** While HAA demonstrates strong results, it's essential to recognize that adversarial defenses are often subject to adversarial attacks. Future research might explore methods to circumvent the HAA framework.
    *   **Subjectivity of Aesthetics:** Aesthetic perception can be subjective and influenced by various factors (cultural background, individual preferences). The current method assumes that certain aesthetic properties are universally favored, which could lead to biases or inconsistencies in the results.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to influence the design of future privacy-preserving content generation techniques. By highlighting the importance of aesthetic cues, it opens up new avenues for developing more effective and robust defense mechanisms.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach to protecting privacy in the context of personalized content generation. Its strength lies in its innovative use of anti-aesthetics and its hierarchical design.  The empirical validation is comprehensive and convincing, indicating a significant improvement over existing methods.  However, the reliance on aesthetic metrics and the potential for circumvention are important limitations to consider. While the work contributes a significant advancement in the field, the issues of computational costs and subjective aesthetic perception are areas requiring further research.

**Score: 8**

A score of 8 reflects the paper's significant novelty and impact, strong empirical validation, and potential influence on the field, balanced by its reliance on specific aesthetic metrics and the inherent challenges of adversarial defense.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Multilingual Contextualization of Large Language Models for Document-Level Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12140v1)
- **Authors**: Miguel Moura Ramos, Patrick Fernandes, Sweta Agrawal, André F. T. Martins
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance in sentence-level machine translation, but scaling to document-level translation remains challenging, particularly in modeling long-range dependencies and discourse phenomena across sentences and paragraphs. In this work, we propose a method to improve LLM-based long-document translation through targeted fine-tuning on high-quality document-level data, which we curate and introduce as DocBlocks. Our approach supports multiple translation paradigms, including direct document-to-document and chunk-level translation, by integrating instructions both with and without surrounding context. This enables models to better capture cross-sentence dependencies while maintaining strong sentence-level translation performance. Experimental results show that incorporating multiple translation paradigms improves document-level translation quality and inference speed compared to prompting and agent-based methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of document-level machine translation (MT) using large language models (LLMs). The authors introduce DOCBLOCKS, a new high-quality, document-level parallel dataset sourced from diverse domains. They propose a fine-tuning approach to improve LLMs' document-level translation capabilities by training them on various instructions, including full documents, contextualized chunks, and sentence-level examples. Experiments demonstrate that this fine-tuning method, combined with prompt-based context modeling, improves document-level translation quality and inference speed compared to prompting and agent-based methods, while maintaining robust sentence-level performance. The study evaluates the approach using multiple datasets, language pairs, and LLM architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in the introduction of DOCBLOCKS, a meticulously curated document-level MT dataset. While fine-tuning LLMs for MT is not entirely new, the focus on document-level context and the multi-faceted training paradigms are incremental innovations. The integration of chunking and quality estimation methods is a relatively standard practice.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a recognized gap in LLM-based MT research, specifically the difficulty in scaling to document-level translation where coherence and long-range dependencies are crucial. By providing a high-quality dataset and a practical fine-tuning methodology, the paper offers a tangible advancement for researchers and practitioners in the field. The comparative evaluation against prompting and agent-based approaches is valuable.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Dataset Contribution:** DOCBLOCKS fills a need for high-quality document-level data and could become a valuable resource for the MT community.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study thoroughly evaluates the proposed approach across multiple datasets, language pairs, and LLM architectures, providing a solid foundation for the claims.
    * **Multi-paradigm Training:** The use of varied training paradigms (full documents, chunks, sentence pairs) is a valuable strategy that leverages the strengths of each.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, with a clear structure and detailed explanations of the methods and results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While practical, the core fine-tuning methodology isn't groundbreaking. Fine-tuning LLMs for MT is well-established, and the modifications in this paper are incremental.
    * **Limited Architectural Exploration:** The paper focuses on fine-tuning existing LLMs rather than proposing novel architectures specifically tailored for document-level MT.
    * **Evaluation Metric Limitations:** Acknowledging the limitations of existing document-level evaluation metrics is important, yet relying on d-BLEU and d-COMET still leaves room for alternative evaluations with more robust metrics.
    * **Computational Cost:** The computational costs of training and inference, although mentioned, require more detailed analysis and comparison with the baselines.

* **Impact:** The paper's impact will depend on the adoption of DOCBLOCKS by the research community. If the dataset proves valuable and leads to further research in document-level MT, the paper's significance will increase. The fine-tuning methodology provides a practical starting point for researchers to explore further.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a new dataset and demonstrating a practical approach to improving document-level MT with LLMs. However, the incremental nature of the core fine-tuning methodology and certain limitations in evaluation and architectural exploration prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mapping Controversies Using Artificial Intelligence: An Analysis of the Hamas-Israel Conflict on YouTube
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12177v1)
- **Authors**: Victor Manuel Hernandez Lopez, Jaime E. Cuellar
- **Abstract**: This article analyzes the Hamas-Israel controversy through 253,925 Spanish-language YouTube comments posted between October 2023 and January 2024, following the October 7 attack that escalated the conflict. Adopting an interdisciplinary approach, the study combines the analysis of controversies from Science and Technology Studies (STS) with advanced computational methodologies, specifically Natural Language Processing (NLP) using the BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) model. Using this approach, the comments were automatically classified into seven categories, reflecting pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, anti- Palestinian, anti-Israeli positions, among others. The results show a predominance of pro- Palestinian comments, although pro-Israeli and anti-Palestinian comments received more "likes." This study also applies the agenda-setting theory to demonstrate how media coverage significantly influences public perception, observing a notable shift in public opinion, transitioning from a pro- Palestinian stance to a more critical position towards Israel. This work highlights the importance of combining social science perspectives with technological tools in the analysis of controversies, presenting a methodological innovation by integrating computational analysis with critical social theories to address complex public opinion phenomena and media narratives.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper, "Mapping Controversies Using Artificial Intelligence: An Analysis of the Hamas-Israel Conflict on YouTube," analyzes the controversy surrounding the Hamas-Israel conflict by examining 253,925 Spanish-language YouTube comments posted between October 2023 and January 2024. It employs an interdisciplinary approach, combining Science and Technology Studies (STS) and computational methodologies, particularly Natural Language Processing (NLP) using the BERT model. The study automatically classifies comments into seven categories (pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, anti-Palestinian, anti-Israeli, etc.) and applies agenda-setting theory to explore how media coverage influences public perception. The results reveal a predominance of pro-Palestinian comments initially, transitioning to a more anti-Israel sentiment later, along with a higher "like" count for pro-Israeli and anti-Palestinian viewpoints. The authors argue for the importance of combining social science perspectives with technological tools for controversy analysis, presenting a methodological innovation by integrating computational analysis with critical social theories to address complex public opinion.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper is moderate. While applying computational methods like BERT to analyze social media commentary is becoming more common, the specific combination of STS principles and agenda-setting theory offers a slightly innovative framework. The focus on Spanish-language comments adds a unique linguistic dimension, as most existing research analyzes English content. However, the core methods are not entirely new.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper lies in its potential to provide a nuanced understanding of public opinion regarding the Hamas-Israel conflict, particularly within the Spanish-speaking world. By identifying prevalent viewpoints and analyzing the shift in sentiment over time, the study offers valuable insights for policymakers, media outlets, and researchers interested in conflict resolution and public diplomacy. The use of YouTube comments as a data source is particularly relevant, given the platform's widespread use and influence.

**Strengths:**

*   **Interdisciplinary Approach:** Combining STS, computational methods, and agenda-setting theory provides a more holistic perspective on the controversy.
*   **Large Dataset:** Analyzing a substantial number of YouTube comments strengthens the reliability and generalizability of the findings.
*   **Contextual Analysis:**  The inclusion of contextual analysis (linking shifts in sentiment with media coverage and events on the ground) provides richer insights than a purely computational approach would.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper describes its methods clearly.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The focus on Spanish-language comments may limit the generalizability of the findings to other linguistic and cultural contexts.
*   **Bias in YouTube Comments:**  YouTube comments are known to be unrepresentative of the general population. Furthermore, bot activity and coordinated campaigns can significantly skew results. The paper does not explicitly address these issues, which could undermine the validity of the analysis.
*   **Oversimplification of Categories:** The classification of comments into pre-defined categories (pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, etc.) may oversimplify the complexity and nuance of individual opinions.
*   **Lack of Critical Engagement with the BERT Model:** While the paper mentions potential biases of the BERT model, it doesn't delve into how these biases might be influencing the results. A deeper critical engagement with the model's limitations would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Limited Validation:** Without additional validation measures (e.g., comparing with surveys or qualitative interviews), it is difficult to ascertain the extent to which YouTube comments accurately reflect broader public opinion.

**Justification:**

The paper shows some promise but is not groundbreaking. The interdisciplinary approach, contextual analysis, and focus on Spanish-language comments contribute to a moderately novel study. However, the limitations related to the representativeness of YouTube data, the simplification of complex opinions, and the lack of critical engagement with the BERT model restrain its overall impact. As such, the assigned score is somewhat above average but not a high one, needing further development for enhanced reliability and deeper engagement with the nuances of both social and technological complexities in digital opinion analysis.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Trusting CHATGPT: how minor tweaks in the prompts lead to major differences in sentiment classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12180v1)
- **Authors**: Jaime E. Cuellar, Oscar Moreno-Martinez, Paula Sofia Torres-Rodriguez, Jaime Andres Pavlich-Mariscal, Andres Felipe Mican-Castiblanco, Juan Guillermo Torres-Hurtado
- **Abstract**: One fundamental question for the social sciences today is: how much can we trust highly complex predictive models like ChatGPT? This study tests the hypothesis that subtle changes in the structure of prompts do not produce significant variations in the classification results of sentiment polarity analysis generated by the Large Language Model GPT-4o mini. Using a dataset of 100.000 comments in Spanish on four Latin American presidents, the model classified the comments as positive, negative, or neutral on 10 occasions, varying the prompts slightly each time. The experimental methodology included exploratory and confirmatory analyses to identify significant discrepancies among classifications. The results reveal that even minor modifications to prompts such as lexical, syntactic, or modal changes, or even their lack of structure impact the classifications. In certain cases, the model produced inconsistent responses, such as mixing categories, providing unsolicited explanations, or using languages other than Spanish. Statistical analysis using Chi-square tests confirmed significant differences in most comparisons between prompts, except in one case where linguistic structures were highly similar. These findings challenge the robustness and trust of Large Language Models for classification tasks, highlighting their vulnerability to variations in instructions. Moreover, it was evident that the lack of structured grammar in prompts increases the frequency of hallucinations. The discussion underscores that trust in Large Language Models is based not only on technical performance but also on the social and institutional relationships underpinning their use.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided paper, titled "¿Confiar en ChatGPT? Cuando una variación sutil en el prompt puede modificar significativamente los resultados" (Trusting ChatGPT? When a subtle variation in the prompt can significantly modify the results). Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness of ChatGPT (specifically, GPT-4o mini) in the context of sentiment analysis in Spanish. It tests the hypothesis that subtle variations in prompt wording can significantly impact the results of sentiment classification. The authors conduct an experiment using 100,000 YouTube comments in Spanish related to four Latin American presidents. They use ten slightly different prompts to classify the comments as positive, negative, or neutral. The methodology involves collecting and structuring the data, designing different prompts (varying lexically, syntactically, modally, and structurally), processing the data using the OpenAI API, and performing quantitative analysis (PCA, Levenshtein distance, and Chi-squared tests) to identify significant discrepancies. The findings reveal that even minor prompt modifications influence the classification results. The study also notes instances of inconsistencies, such as mixing categories or using non-Spanish language.  The authors conclude that the reliability of LLMs for classification tasks is questionable, and the trust placed in these models must extend beyond their technical performance to include the social and institutional relationships that underpin their use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper touches upon a crucial issue in the application of LLMs. While the core idea – that prompts influence LLM outputs – isn't entirely new, its systematic examination of this sensitivity in the context of *sentiment analysis specifically in Spanish*, using quantitative methods to compare a set of deliberately tweaked prompts, adds a layer of granularity. This is a valuable contribution, as many existing studies may not focus *specifically* on prompt engineering and its impact on sentiment analysis robustness across variations. Focusing on Spanish sentiment also adds novelty, as much prompt engineering research is focused on English.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for researchers and practitioners who rely on LLMs for sentiment analysis and other classification tasks. Demonstrating that even subtle changes in prompts can lead to statistically significant differences in results raises concerns about the reproducibility and reliability of studies using LLMs.  It suggests that prompt design is not merely a technical detail but a critical methodological consideration. This has practical implications for designing sentiment analysis workflows with more robust prompts. The theoretical discussion of trust in LLMs, highlighting the need to consider both technical and social/institutional dimensions, adds depth to the analysis. The paper also emphasizes the importance of understanding inherent uncertainties and limitations in LLMs, prompting users to be more cautious in their reliance on their output.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined experimental setup, including data collection, prompt design, and statistical analysis. The use of multiple quantitative measures (PCA, Levenshtein distance, Chi-squared tests) strengthens the validity of the results.
    *   **Empirical evidence:** The large dataset and systematic experimentation provide robust evidence for the claims made.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly written, making it accessible to a wide audience.
    *   **Practical Relevance**: Highlighted practical implication of prompt design needing to be methodological considerations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Scope Limitation:** The study uses YouTube comment data. While valuable, this data source may not be representative of all types of text data used for sentiment analysis. The conclusion about LLMs not being ready could be strengthened by including other types of text data (such as news articles or customer reviews).

    *   **GPT-4o mini.** Use of a smaller mini LLM may result in the effect of prompts on robustness being overestimated when compared to much larger and more modern LLMs.

    *   **Limited ground truth comparison**: While the paper makes a sound call for future comparisons with ground truth, the novelty could be improved by incorporating it and further bolstering the results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLMs, especially in the areas of prompt engineering, robustness evaluation, and ethical considerations. It highlights the need for more careful attention to prompt design and the limitations of current LLMs, encouraging the development of more robust and reliable models. Also it calls for a greater awareness on inherent limitations of LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by systematically investigating the impact of subtle prompt variations on sentiment analysis results using LLMs. It provides empirical evidence for the sensitivity of LLMs to prompt wording, raising important concerns about the reliability of these models. While it does have limitations in terms of data scope and exploration with comparing LLMs with ground truth, the paper offers insights into prompt engineering, evaluation, ethical considerations and practical relevance to LLMs.
