### SALAD: Improving Robustness and Generalization through Contrastive Learning with Structure-Aware and LLM-Driven Augmented Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12185v1)
- **Authors**: Suyoung Bae, Hyojun Kim, YunSeok Choi, Jee-Hyong Lee
- **Abstract**: In various natural language processing (NLP) tasks, fine-tuning Pre-trained Language Models (PLMs) often leads to the issue of spurious correlations, which negatively impacts performance, particularly when dealing with out-of-distribution data. To address this problem, we propose SALAD}(Structure Aware and LLM-driven Augmented Data), a novel approach designed to enhance model robustness and generalization by generating structure-aware and counterfactually augmented data for contrastive learning. Our method leverages a tagging-based approach to generate structure-aware positive samples and utilizes large language models (LLMs) to generate counterfactual negative samples with diverse sentence patterns. By applying contrastive learning, SALAD enables the model to focus on learning the structural relationships between key sentence components while minimizing reliance on spurious correlations. We validate our approach through experiments on three tasks: Sentiment Classification, Sexism Detection, and Natural Language Inference. The results demonstrate that SALAD not only improves model robustness and performance across different environments but also enhances generalization to out-of-distribution datasets and cross-domain scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SALAD, a novel approach to improve the robustness and generalization of pre-trained language models (PLMs) in NLP tasks. SALAD addresses the issue of models relying on spurious correlations by generating structure-aware and counterfactually augmented data for contrastive learning. The method uses a tagging-based approach to generate positive samples that preserve structural patterns while masking non-causal words.  It also leverages LLMs to generate diverse negative samples that flip the labels but retain contextual variation.  The paper validates SALAD through experiments on Sentiment Classification, Sexism Detection, and Natural Language Inference, showing improvements in robustness and generalization across different environments and datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines known techniques (data augmentation, contrastive learning, structural analysis) in a novel way to address a significant problem (spurious correlations). The key novelty lies in the integrated approach of tagging-based structure-aware augmentation for positive samples *and* LLM-driven counterfactual data generation for negative samples, specifically designed to encourage the model to learn structural relationships. While each component has been explored previously, their synergistic combination constitutes a non-trivial contribution.

*   **Significance:** Spurious correlations are a major obstacle to reliable NLP model deployment, especially in real-world scenarios where data distributions can vary significantly. Addressing this issue with a robust and generalizable method is therefore important. The results presented indicate that SALAD is indeed effective in improving model robustness and generalization, which has the potential to impact various NLP applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear and well-motivated explanation of the problem and the proposed solution.
    *   The proposed SALAD method is well-defined and reasonably easy to implement.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, covering three different NLP tasks and using multiple datasets for evaluation, including out-of-distribution and cross-domain settings.
    *   Ablation studies are included to analyze the contribution of different components of SALAD.
    *   The analysis of the LLM-generated data provides insights into the method's effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT models (albeit a smaller one, GPT-40-mini) might raise concerns about accessibility and reproducibility, although the prompt is simple.  Consideration of more readily available or open-source LLMs, or providing detailed instructions to replicate the results with different LLMs, would strengthen the paper.
    *   While the experimental results are promising, the absolute performance gains are relatively modest compared to the state-of-the-art, particularly for tasks like sentiment classification where existing models are already quite accurate. However, the improvement in *robustness* is a more significant contribution.
    *   The parameter tuning seems computationally expensive.  More efficient strategies for setting hyperparameters could be explored.
    *   The scope of the experiments could be expanded to more diverse NLP tasks and larger-scale datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The approach is interesting and potentially broadly applicable.  The clear motivation, detailed experimental setup, and code availability (if released) could encourage other researchers to adopt and extend the method. The combination of structural awareness and LLM-driven augmentation might inspire future research in similar directions.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

SALAD tackles a critical problem in NLP and presents a novel combination of techniques that demonstrably improves robustness and generalization. The experimental evaluation is thorough, and the results are promising. However, the absolute performance gains could be more substantial, and reliance on an external language model and the associated accessibility issues somewhat limit its practicality. The paper is well-written and contributes a valuable approach to the field, but there's room for improvement regarding the accessibility of the method and magnitude of the performance gains.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### What Do Large Language Models Know? Tacit Knowledge as a Potential Causal-Explanatory Structure
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12187v1)
- **Authors**: Céline Budding
- **Abstract**: It is sometimes assumed that Large Language Models (LLMs) know language, or for example that they know that Paris is the capital of France. But what -- if anything -- do LLMs actually know? In this paper, I argue that LLMs can acquire tacit knowledge as defined by Martin Davies (1990). Whereas Davies himself denies that neural networks can acquire tacit knowledge, I demonstrate that certain architectural features of LLMs satisfy the constraints of semantic description, syntactic structure, and causal systematicity. Thus, tacit knowledge may serve as a conceptual framework for describing, explaining, and intervening on LLMs and their behavior.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, followed by a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper argues that Large Language Models (LLMs) can acquire tacit knowledge, as defined by Martin Davies. While Davies himself doubted that neural networks could possess tacit knowledge, the author contends that certain architectural features of LLMs, especially the embedding layer, satisfy the necessary constraints of semantic description, causal systematicity, and a modified version of syntactic structure (weakened from Davies' original strict requirement). The paper uses the concept of key-value pairs to establish causal common factors and supports this claim by analyzing recent work by Meng et al. and how interventions demonstrate the desired properties of knowledge and systematicity. The overall goal is to offer a conceptual framework for describing, explaining, and potentially intervening on LLMs and their behavior through the lens of tacit knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Conceptual Framework:** The paper provides a well-defined conceptual framework using Davies' notion of tacit knowledge. It is able to clearly explain why a neural network can be said to "know" something without necessarily having explicit representations.
    *   **Addressing a Key Question:** The paper tackles the fundamental question of what LLMs actually *learn* during training. This is a central concern in the field and beyond, impacting issues of bias, trust, and ethical considerations.
    *   **Architectural Focus:** The paper effectively links architectural components (especially the embedding layer and the MLP layers, in the work of Meng et al.) to the conceptual framework.  This connection to the physical structure of the model enables concrete analysis and allows the construction of testable hypotheses.
    *   **Nuanced Argument:** The author acknowledges Davies' original concerns about neural networks meeting the syntactic structure constraint and persuasively argues for a weaker, more relevant version applicable to LLMs.
    *   **Intervention-Based Analysis:** By focusing on the intervention-based work of Meng et al., the paper connects its theoretical argument to concrete experimental findings, which lends support to its claims.
    *   **Acknowledges Limitations:** The paper explicitly acknowledges the limitations of the analysis and the preliminary nature of the evidence.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Empirical Scope:** The argument relies heavily on the findings of a specific study (Meng et al.). While insightful, generalizing from one paper's findings to all LLMs might be too broad a conclusion. It only establishes one example.
    *   **Causal Claim Issues**: Causality, as measured in Meng et. al and generally in deep learning models, is a hot topic of debate. Causal tracing and ROME are potentially controversial methods that are subject to debate. Hase et al. has shown that the methods are limited in scope.
    *   **Interpretation of Causal Systematicity:** The interpretation of causal systematicity, while clearly articulated, could be more rigorously defended. Are the patterns observed by Meng et al. truly reflective of underlying causal processes, or do they arise from other factors like statistical biases or unintended model properties?

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The application of Davies' tacit knowledge framework to LLMs is novel and provides a valuable lens for understanding these systems. The core contribution is conceptual, offering a new way to *think* about LLMs.
    *   The weakening of the syntactic structure constraint to align with the specific architecture of LLMs is a significant adaptation of Davies' framework, which opens the door to potentially attributing knowledge to a broader range of neural network models.
    *   By linking this framework to intervention-based studies, the paper suggests new avenues for research in mechanistic interpretability and model editing, making the argument impactful on how we *study* LLMs as well.
    *   While the experimental support is somewhat limited, the paper serves as a compelling starting point for further investigation. The overall impact of this paper hinges on the future validation and extension of these claims.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a novel and insightful conceptual framework for understanding LLMs, backed by a careful analysis of existing research and a keen awareness of limitations. While the empirical basis could be stronger and the causal claims may be dubious, it represents a valuable contribution to the field by offering a fresh perspective on the nature of knowledge in these complex systems. It reframes debates in Philosophy of Mind for the current era of LLMs.

### d1: Scaling Reasoning in Diffusion Large Language Models via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12216v1)
- **Authors**: Siyan Zhao, Devaansh Gupta, Qinqing Zheng, Aditya Grover
- **Abstract**: Recent large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong reasoning capabilities that benefits from online reinforcement learning (RL). These capabilities have primarily been demonstrated within the left-to-right autoregressive (AR) generation paradigm. In contrast, non-autoregressive paradigms based on diffusion generate text in a coarse-to-fine manner. Although recent diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs) have achieved competitive language modeling performance compared to their AR counterparts, it remains unclear if dLLMs can also leverage recent advances in LLM reasoning. To this end, we propose d1, a framework to adapt pre-trained masked dLLMs into reasoning models via a combination of supervised finetuning (SFT) and RL. Specifically, we develop and extend techniques to improve reasoning in pretrained dLLMs: (a) we utilize a masked SFT technique to distill knowledge and instill self-improvement behavior directly from existing datasets, and (b) we introduce a novel critic-free, policy-gradient based RL algorithm called diffu-GRPO. Through empirical studies, we investigate the performance of different post-training recipes on multiple mathematical and logical reasoning benchmarks. We find that d1 yields the best performance and significantly improves performance of a state-of-the-art dLLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces d1, a two-stage framework for scaling reasoning capabilities in diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs). d1 combines supervised finetuning (SFT) on high-quality reasoning data with a novel policy gradient reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm called diffu-GRPO.  Diffu-GRPO adapts the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm to the non-autoregressive nature of dLLMs by introducing an efficient one-step estimation of log-probabilities, leveraging random prompt masking. The authors empirically evaluate d1, instantiating it with the LLaDA-8B-Instruct model and testing it on mathematical and logical reasoning benchmarks. Results demonstrate that d1 consistently outperforms baselines, including SFT-only and diffu-GRPO-only approaches. The paper also includes ablation studies and qualitative analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The adaptation of RL techniques, specifically GRPO, to dLLMs is the primary source of novelty. Existing RL methods have primarily focused on autoregressive LLMs. The introduction of diffu-GRPO, designed to handle the non-sequential generation process of dLLMs, addresses a key challenge. The random prompt masking technique for efficient log-probability estimation is also a novel contribution. However, the combination of SFT and RL is a well-established paradigm, reducing the overall novelty somewhat.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important question: Can dLLMs leverage recent advances in LLM reasoning capabilities through RL?  The positive results suggest that dLLMs can indeed benefit from RL, opening up new avenues for research and development.  The performance improvements demonstrated on mathematical and logical reasoning benchmarks provide empirical evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed framework. Moreover, given the computational advantages dLLMs offer (as mentioned in the paper regarding inference efficiency and previously documented), improving reasoning performance of this class of models is valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:**  The paper clearly identifies the gap in applying RL to dLLMs.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The diffu-GRPO algorithm is well-motivated and explained.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The empirical evaluation is thorough, including comparisons with multiple baselines, ablation studies, and qualitative analysis.
    *   **Addresses Computational Challenges:**  The introduction of efficient log-probability estimation via prompt masking helps mitigate the computational burden of RL in dLLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:**  The evaluation is limited to mathematical and logical reasoning tasks. It is unclear how well d1 would perform on other reasoning tasks, such as commonsense reasoning or natural language understanding. Further experimentation on different benchmark datasets is needed.
    *   **Base Model Choice:** The choice of LLaDA-8B-Instruct is reasonable, but the conclusions may not generalize to all dLLMs. It would be beneficial to evaluate d1 with other dLLM architectures.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks theoretical justification for the effectiveness of diffu-GRPO and the random prompt masking technique. While the empirical results are positive, a theoretical understanding could provide deeper insights.
    *  **Incremental Advance:** The core idea of adapting GRPO to dLLMs, although valuable, doesn't represent a paradigm shift in the broader landscape of RL and LLMs. It's more of a clever engineering adaptation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could encourage further research into RL for dLLMs.  The diffu-GRPO algorithm and the efficient log-probability estimation technique could serve as building blocks for future work. However, the reliance on SFT as a necessary precursor might limit its applicability in scenarios where high-quality reasoning data is scarce.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field by successfully adapting RL techniques to dLLMs, addressing a significant gap in the existing literature. The introduced diffu-GRPO algorithm, coupled with the efficient estimation method, enables effective training. While the novelty is incremental, the thorough empirical evaluation and demonstrated performance improvements warrant a score above average. However, the limited scope of evaluation and the lack of theoretical analysis prevent a higher rating.

### Coding-Prior Guided Diffusion Network for Video Deblurring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12222v1)
- **Authors**: Yike Liu, Jianhui Zhang, Haipeng Li, Shuaicheng Liu, Bing Zeng
- **Abstract**: While recent video deblurring methods have advanced significantly, they often overlook two valuable prior information: (1) motion vectors (MVs) and coding residuals (CRs) from video codecs, which provide efficient inter-frame alignment cues, and (2) the rich real-world knowledge embedded in pre-trained diffusion generative models. We present CPGDNet, a novel two-stage framework that effectively leverages both coding priors and generative diffusion priors for high-quality deblurring. First, our coding-prior feature propagation (CPFP) module utilizes MVs for efficient frame alignment and CRs to generate attention masks, addressing motion inaccuracies and texture variations. Second, a coding-prior controlled generation (CPC) module network integrates coding priors into a pretrained diffusion model, guiding it to enhance critical regions and synthesize realistic details. Experiments demonstrate our method achieves state-of-the-art perceptual quality with up to 30% improvement in IQA metrics. Both the code and the codingprior-augmented dataset will be open-sourced.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided text and am ready to offer a summary and critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary**

The paper introduces CPGD-Net, a novel two-stage framework designed for video deblurring. The core idea is to leverage two types of prior information that have been largely overlooked in previous work: coding priors (motion vectors and coding residuals extracted directly from compressed video streams) and generative diffusion priors (knowledge embedded in pre-trained diffusion models). The first stage, the coding-prior feature propagation (CPFP) module, uses motion vectors for frame alignment and coding residuals to generate attention masks, which aims to address motion inaccuracies and texture variations. The second stage, the coding-prior controlled generation (CPC) module, integrates coding priors into a pre-trained diffusion model, guiding it to enhance critical regions and synthesize realistic details. The authors claim that this approach achieves state-of-the-art perceptual quality, as demonstrated through experiments on benchmark datasets. The authors also augment standard benchmark datasets with corresponding video coding information. The authors state the code and coding-prior-augmented dataset will be open-sourced.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty*:
The novelty of this paper lies in its **holistic integration** of coding priors from compressed video streams with generative diffusion models for video deblurring.  While individual components such as optical flow and attention mechanisms have been explored in previous deblurring methods, the **explicit and combined use of MVs and coding residuals alongside diffusion models is a relatively novel approach**. Specifically, the CPFP module appears to be a unique contribution, as is the controlled injection of coding priors into the attention mechanism of the diffusion model. The integration of compressed video codec data is generally less explored than simply utilizing the decoded frames. The augmentation of existing benchmark datasets with coding prior information also adds a new dimension to the experimental evaluation in this field. However, It's crucial to verify whether the benefits truly stem from the coding priors or simply from a carefully tuned architecture incorporating a pre-trained diffusion model.

*Significance*:
The significance of the paper is twofold. First, it addresses a practical problem: deblurring videos that are often compressed, making the use of readily available coding information sensible.  Second, it offers a potential pathway for improving video deblurring performance by exploiting information that is inherent to video encoding without resorting to computationally expensive optical flow estimation. If the results hold up under scrutiny, it could shift the paradigm in video deblurring research, encouraging the use of coding artifacts as a source of useful prior information. It shows a potential method for combining generative AI and compression.

*Strengths*:
*   The approach is well-motivated, recognizing the importance of both motion information and detail preservation.
*   The two-stage architecture is logical, first performing alignment and initial restoration, and then refining the output with generative priors.
*   The integration of coding priors into the attention mechanism of the diffusion model is an innovative idea.
*   The experimental results, though showing lower PSNR/SSIM,  indicate superior perceptual quality according to no-reference IQA metrics.
*   The paper claims to open-source their code and augmented dataset, enhancing reproducibility and furthering research.

*Weaknesses*:
*   The decrease in PSNR/SSIM scores, while acknowledged by the authors, is a point of concern. It raises questions about whether the improvement in perceptual quality comes at the expense of fidelity to the original scene.  It requires careful justification and possibly further analysis of the types of errors being introduced.
*   The experimental section lacks comparative ablation studies. While the authors perform ablation studies on their architecture they don't adequately compare the performance of utilizing MVs versus the additional computation of utilizing optical flow. This raises questions about whether the same results can be achieved in more traditional approaches with additional computation.
*   The quantitative improvement over previous methods (as indicated by IQA metrics) needs to be significantly more substantial to truly claim a breakthrough. The stated improvement over previous no-reference IQA metrics is up to 30%.
*   The paper needs to more rigourously discuss in detail the computational cost of incorporating the coding priors as compared to traditional approaches.

*Potential Influence*:
The paper has the potential to influence video deblurring research by highlighting the value of coding priors and demonstrating a novel way to integrate them with generative models. It could lead to more efficient and perceptually pleasing deblurring algorithms. However, its impact will depend on the reproducibility of the results, the generalizability of the approach to different video codecs and content, and how easily it can be adapted and extended by other researchers.

*Justification for Score*:
Considering the novelty of the approach, its potential significance, and the strengths of the experimental results, but also acknowledging the concerns about PSNR/SSIM scores and the lack of more robust analysis of computational cost, I'd assign a score of **7**. It is a promising paper with a novel idea, but needs more rigorous validation and comparison to reach a score of 8 or higher. The claims of greatly improved perceptual quality need more backing, and the paper needs to make it easier for other researchers to implement and evaluate it.

### Watermarking Needs Input Repetition Masking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12229v1)
- **Authors**: David Khachaturov, Robert Mullins, Ilia Shumailov, Sumanth Dathathri
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) raised concerns over potential misuse, such as for spreading misinformation. In response two counter measures emerged: machine learning-based detectors that predict if text is synthetic, and LLM watermarking, which subtly marks generated text for identification and attribution. Meanwhile, humans are known to adjust language to their conversational partners both syntactically and lexically. By implication, it is possible that humans or unwatermarked LLMs could unintentionally mimic properties of LLM generated text, making counter measures unreliable. In this work we investigate the extent to which such conversational adaptation happens. We call the concept $\textit{mimicry}$ and demonstrate that both humans and LLMs end up mimicking, including the watermarking signal even in seemingly improbable settings. This challenges current academic assumptions and suggests that for long-term watermarking to be reliable, the likelihood of false positives needs to be significantly lower, while longer word sequences should be used for seeding watermarking mechanisms.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces the concept of "watermark mimicry" in large language models (LLMs), where unwatermarked LLMs, through conversation with watermarked LLMs, begin to unintentionally mimic the statistical properties of the watermarking signal. The authors demonstrate, using both LLM-LLM and human-LLM interactions, that such mimicry can occur, potentially undermining the reliability of current LLM watermarking schemes. They highlight the need for future watermarking techniques to account for this adaptation and to provide lower false positive rates and consider longer word sequences for seeding watermarking mechanisms. They test this hypothesis using a smaller 7b and 13b model versus a larger GPT-40 model. This testing shows the 7b and 13b models mimic the watermark, whereas the GPT-40 models does not, hypothesizing that the increased modeling capabilities lead to the lack of mimicry in GPT-40.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of watermark mimicry is reasonably novel. While the general idea of language adaptation in conversations is well-established, its specific application to LLM watermarks and the potential for undermining their effectiveness is a new and important observation. The paper's demonstration that even humans can exhibit this mimicry, as evidenced by increased detection of human text as "LLM-like" by existing detectors, strengthens the novelty of the finding.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the long-term viability of LLM watermarking as a means of identifying and attributing AI-generated content. If unwatermarked LLMs and even humans can inadvertently produce text with watermarking signatures, it raises serious questions about the reliability of watermark detectors and their ability to distinguish between truly synthetic and mimicked content. This could lead to false accusations and undermine the credibility of watermarking as a provenance technique. The findings related to the large language model GPT-40 are also significant because the model exhibits little to no mimicry. The authors appropriately hypothesize that the size and power of a language model will reduce the likelihood of it unintentionally mimicing the watermarks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is clearly written and presents a well-defined problem.
    *   The experiments, while relatively simple, are sufficient to demonstrate the existence of watermark mimicry.
    *   The analysis is thoughtful and identifies the potential mechanisms driving the observed behavior.
    *   The paper appropriately acknowledges the limitations of the current study, such as the limited scope of watermarking schemes and model sizes considered.
    *   There is appropriate ethical consideration of the work.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The experiments could be more rigorous. For example, a more detailed analysis of the specific token sequences being mimicked and their relationship to the watermarking algorithm would strengthen the argument.
    *   The models are all on the smaller side. While a larger model is examined, GPT-4o, it is only examined in the conversation case of watermarked -> unwatermarked LLM. Furthermore, given that the smaller model is not a state-of-the-art watermarking scheme, it is difficult to generalize the findings to all watermarking methods.
    *   The paper could benefit from exploring potential mitigation strategies for watermark mimicry. While suggestions are made, they are not explored empirically.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the design and evaluation of future LLM watermarking schemes. It highlights the importance of considering adaptive behavior and the need for more robust and subtle watermarking techniques that are less susceptible to mimicry. It is also likely to spur further research into the dynamics of language adaptation in human-AI interactions.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially important finding regarding the vulnerability of LLM watermarking schemes to mimicry. The experiments, while not exhaustive, provide compelling evidence for the existence of this phenomenon. While there are limitations, the paper clearly identifies a critical challenge for the field and provides a solid foundation for future research. The relatively small model size and potential limitations in real-world applicability temper the immediate impact, but the paper's conceptual contribution and potential for influencing future research warrant a positive evaluation.

### MOS: Towards Effective Smart Contract Vulnerability Detection through Mixture-of-Experts Tuning of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12234v1)
- **Authors**: Hang Yuan, Lei Yu, Zhirong Huang, Jingyuan Zhang, Junyi Lu, Shiqi Cheng, Li Yang, Fengjun Zhang, Jiajia Ma, Chun Zuo
- **Abstract**: Smart contract vulnerabilities pose significant security risks to blockchain systems, potentially leading to severe financial losses. Existing methods face several limitations: (1) Program analysis-based approaches rely on predefined patterns, lacking flexibility for new vulnerability types; (2) Deep learning-based methods lack explanations; (3) Large language model-based approaches suffer from high false positives. We propose MOS, a smart contract vulnerability detection framework based on mixture-of-experts tuning (MOE-Tuning) of large language models. First, we conduct continual pre-training on a large-scale smart contract dataset to provide domain-enhanced initialization. Second, we construct a high-quality MOE-Tuning dataset through a multi-stage pipeline combining LLM generation and expert verification for reliable explanations. Third, we design a vulnerability-aware routing mechanism that activates the most relevant expert networks by analyzing code features and their matching degree with experts. Finally, we extend the feed-forward layers into multiple parallel expert networks, each specializing in specific vulnerability patterns. We employ a dual-objective loss function: one for optimizing detection and explanation performance, and another for ensuring reasonable distribution of vulnerability types to experts through entropy calculation. Experiments show that MOS significantly outperforms existing methods with average improvements of 6.32% in F1 score and 4.80% in accuracy. The vulnerability explanations achieve positive ratings (scores of 3-4 on a 4-point scale) of 82.96%, 85.21% and 94.58% for correctness, completeness, and conciseness through human and LLM evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MOS: Towards Effective Smart Contract Vulnerability Detection through Mixture-of-Experts Tuning of Large Language Models" introduces a new framework, MOS, for detecting vulnerabilities in smart contracts. It addresses limitations of existing methods (program analysis, deep learning, and LLMs) by employing a Mixture-of-Experts (MOE) tuning approach using Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework includes:

1.  **Continual Pre-training:** A domain-enhanced initialization for the LLM using smart contract data.
2.  **MOE-Tuning Dataset:** A dataset created with LLM generation and expert verification for reliable vulnerability explanations.
3.  **Vulnerability-Aware Routing:** Directing code features to relevant expert networks.
4.  **Specialized MOE Network:** Multiple parallel expert networks specializing in specific vulnerability patterns.
5.  **Dual-Objective Loss Function:** Optimizing both vulnerability detection and explanation.

The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that MOS outperforms existing methods in F1 score and accuracy across various vulnerability types, while also generating high-quality vulnerability explanations. The evaluation process also involves a combined approach using both human and LLM evaluation for better performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of techniques for smart contract vulnerability detection. The use of MOE tuning with LLMs in this context is a significant step forward. Although other approaches exist, MOS’s architecture and training methodology (dual-objective loss, vulnerability-aware routing) offer a distinctive advantage.
*   **Significance:** Smart contract security is a crucial issue in blockchain technology due to immutability and potential for significant financial losses. The proposed framework aims to improve the current security standards. The improvements in detection performance and the generation of explanations contribute valuable tools for developers and auditors. The framework's architecture makes it inherently adaptable to new or emerging threats.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results: The experiments show significant improvements over baselines.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation: Multiple datasets and evaluation metrics are used.
    *   Explanation capability: The framework focuses on providing meaningful explanations, which is important for practical use.
    *   Well-defined architecture: The individual components of the MOS framework are clearly described.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational requirements: The framework requires considerable computational resources for training. This can limit its accessibility. The implementation details about the distributed resources might be helpful to the interested readers.
    *   Prompt engineering sensitivity: Like all LLM-based approaches, the framework's performance might be sensitive to prompt engineering choices.
    *   Dataset bias: Potential bias in the dataset, including the MOE-tuning and evaluation datasets, is a concern. More details on the distribution of vulnerabilities would make the claims much more stronger.
    *   Complexity for adoption: The complexity in architecture may be a possible hinderence for quick adaptation by practitioners. It can also impact explainability and trust to the model, leading to slower real-world adoptions.
*   **Potential influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more effective and explainable approach to smart contract vulnerability detection. The MOE tuning approach and the focus on generating explanations can be adopted by other researchers. The dataset created as part of this work is also a valuable contribution.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper demonstrates a strong understanding of current problems and makes a substantial contribution towards improving the current security standards. The experimental results, architectural choices, and evaluation techniques are well-justified. However, some challenges include the high computational costs, the potential prompt sensitivity, and complexities for practitioners. These limitations prevent it from reaching an exceptionally high score.

### Cobra: Efficient Line Art COlorization with BRoAder References
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12240v1)
- **Authors**: Junhao Zhuang, Lingen Li, Xuan Ju, Zhaoyang Zhang, Chun Yuan, Ying Shan
- **Abstract**: The comic production industry requires reference-based line art colorization with high accuracy, efficiency, contextual consistency, and flexible control. A comic page often involves diverse characters, objects, and backgrounds, which complicates the coloring process. Despite advancements in diffusion models for image generation, their application in line art colorization remains limited, facing challenges related to handling extensive reference images, time-consuming inference, and flexible control. We investigate the necessity of extensive contextual image guidance on the quality of line art colorization. To address these challenges, we introduce Cobra, an efficient and versatile method that supports color hints and utilizes over 200 reference images while maintaining low latency. Central to Cobra is a Causal Sparse DiT architecture, which leverages specially designed positional encodings, causal sparse attention, and Key-Value Cache to effectively manage long-context references and ensure color identity consistency. Results demonstrate that Cobra achieves accurate line art colorization through extensive contextual reference, significantly enhancing inference speed and interactivity, thereby meeting critical industrial demands. We release our codes and models on our project page: https://zhuang2002.github.io/Cobra/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Cobra: Efficient Line Art Colorization with BRoAder References":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Cobra, a novel framework for reference-based line art colorization, specifically designed for comic creation workflows. Cobra aims to improve upon existing methods by addressing limitations related to the number of reference images used, inference speed, and the ability to handle complex, context-dependent comic pages. The core innovations of Cobra include: (1) Multi-Identity Consistency through spatial concatenation of clean reference image latents and noise latents using attention; (2) Efficient Attention Design with Causal Sparse DiT architecture (Causal Sparse Attention and Key-Value Cache) to reduce computational complexity of full attention; (3) Flexible Position Encoding (Localized Reusable Position Encoding) that reuses localized encodings; and (4) Color Hint Integration for flexible user control. The paper also introduces Cobra-bench, a new benchmark for multi-reference-based comic line art colorization. The authors demonstrate that Cobra achieves state-of-the-art results in terms of image quality, color ID accuracy, and inference efficiency, particularly when using a large number of reference images.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several innovative architectural components. The Causal Sparse DiT represents a significant step forward in efficient attention mechanisms for image colorization, allowing for the effective utilization of a large number of reference images (over 200), a limitation of previous methods. The Localized Reusable Position Encoding is also a novel approach to handle spatial concatenation efficiently and effectively by maintaining spatial proximity for positional encodings even with many references.

*   **Significance:** The addressed problem of line art colorization is important in the comic production industry. Cobra appears to improve upon existing limitations, with faster inference and better accuracy. The new Cobra-bench benchmark is valuable as it provides a standardized dataset for evaluating and comparing methods in this domain. The qualitative and quantitative results presented in the paper appear convincing, demonstrating the superiority of Cobra over existing approaches. The user study further strengthens the claim of improved performance. However, a clear weakness highlighted is its performance in generalizing to different characters outside of the training domain.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The technical innovations (Causal Sparse DiT, Localized Reusable Position Encoding) are well-motivated and explained.
    *   The quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate significant improvements over existing methods.
    *   The introduction of Cobra-bench is a valuable contribution to the community.
    *   The paper addresses practical challenges in comic production.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The limitation concerning style transfer or generalizing between different characters is a substantial weakness, indicating a domain restriction.
    *   While the paper highlights efficiency gains, a more detailed analysis of memory usage and computational cost compared to other methods would be beneficial.
    *   More details could be provided on the training data curation process.
    *   The writing quality could be improved for clarity, particularly in explaining some of the technical details.

*   **Potential Influence:** Cobra has the potential to significantly impact the comic production industry by enabling more efficient and accurate colorization workflows. The proposed Causal Sparse Attention could also be applied to other image generation and editing tasks that involve large contextual references. The Cobra-bench dataset will also promote further research in this field.

Considering these points, while the character identity is still a limitation, the gains in terms of using more reference images with efficiency and maintained color ID is substantial. Therefore:

**Score: 7**

The paper offers significant innovation and demonstrates strong performance within its target domain. The limitations regarding character identity transfer prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SIDME: Self-supervised Image Demoiréing via Masked Encoder-Decoder Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12245v1)
- **Authors**: Xia Wang, Haiyang Sun, Tiantian Cao, Yueying Sun, Min Feng
- **Abstract**: Moir\'e patterns, resulting from aliasing between object light signals and camera sampling frequencies, often degrade image quality during capture. Traditional demoir\'eing methods have generally treated images as a whole for processing and training, neglecting the unique signal characteristics of different color channels. Moreover, the randomness and variability of moir\'e pattern generation pose challenges to the robustness of existing methods when applied to real-world data. To address these issues, this paper presents SIDME (Self-supervised Image Demoir\'eing via Masked Encoder-Decoder Reconstruction), a novel model designed to generate high-quality visual images by effectively processing moir\'e patterns. SIDME combines a masked encoder-decoder architecture with self-supervised learning, allowing the model to reconstruct images using the inherent properties of camera sampling frequencies. A key innovation is the random masked image reconstructor, which utilizes an encoder-decoder structure to handle the reconstruction task. Furthermore, since the green channel in camera sampling has a higher sampling frequency compared to red and blue channels, a specialized self-supervised loss function is designed to improve the training efficiency and effectiveness. To ensure the generalization ability of the model, a self-supervised moir\'e image generation method has been developed to produce a dataset that closely mimics real-world conditions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SIDME outperforms existing methods in processing real moir\'e pattern data, showing its superior generalization performance and robustness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SIDME (Self-supervised Image Demoiréing via Masked Encoder-Decoder Reconstruction), a novel self-supervised approach for removing moiré patterns from images. SIDME uses a masked encoder-decoder architecture combined with a self-supervised learning strategy. A key innovation is the random masked image reconstructor, leveraging the encoder-decoder structure to handle the reconstruction task effectively. The paper also exploits the higher sampling frequency of the green channel in camera sensors by designing a specialized self-supervised loss function. Finally, they introduce a self-supervised moiré image generation method to create a realistic training dataset. Experimental results show that SIDME outperforms existing methods in processing real moiré pattern data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques, but the novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking. Masked autoencoders are not new, nor are self-supervised learning strategies. The specific adaptation of these ideas to the moiré removal problem, combined with the exploitation of the green channel's properties, constitutes the primary source of novelty. While the moiré image generation process for creating realistic training data is a useful contribution, it builds upon existing approaches.

*   **Significance:** The problem of moiré pattern removal is practically relevant, and the paper addresses a limitation of previous methods by focusing on real-world data and leveraging the properties of camera sensors. The performance gains demonstrated over existing methods are meaningful, indicating a step forward in the field. However, the practical applicability and robustness of the approach need further validation on a wider range of real-world datasets and scenarios. The self-supervised training offers practical advantages.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-designed architecture that combines established and custom techniques.
    *   Effective exploitation of green channel characteristics.
    *   Realistic moiré image generation method.
    *   Comprehensive experimental results that demonstrate superior performance compared to existing methods on both synthetic and real-world data.
    *   Ablation studies that validate the effectiveness of the different components of the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty is incremental and relies on existing techniques (masked autoencoders, self-supervised learning).
    *   The computational complexity of the proposed method is not explicitly discussed.
    *   Limited analysis of failure cases or limitations of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more effective and robust moiré removal algorithms. The self-supervised training approach and the exploitation of camera sensor characteristics are valuable insights that could be adopted by other researchers.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper is technically sound and presents a useful contribution to the field, the lack of truly groundbreaking novelty limits its impact. The approach builds upon existing techniques and adapts them to the specific problem of moiré removal. The experimental results demonstrate meaningful improvements over existing methods, but further validation on a wider range of real-world data and scenarios is needed.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Comparative Evaluation of Radiomics and Deep Learning Models for Disease Detection in Chest Radiography
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12249v1)
- **Authors**: Zhijin He, Alan B. McMillan
- **Abstract**: The application of artificial intelligence (AI) in medical imaging has revolutionized diagnostic practices, enabling advanced analysis and interpretation of radiological data. This study presents a comprehensive evaluation of radiomics-based and deep learning-based approaches for disease detection in chest radiography, focusing on COVID-19, lung opacity, and viral pneumonia. While deep learning models, particularly convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and vision transformers (ViTs), learn directly from image data, radiomics-based models extract and analyze quantitative features, potentially providing advantages in data-limited scenarios. This study systematically compares the diagnostic accuracy and robustness of various AI models, including Decision Trees, Gradient Boosting, Random Forests, Support Vector Machines (SVM), and Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLP) for radiomics, against state-of-the-art computer vision deep learning architectures. Performance metrics across varying sample sizes reveal insights into each model's efficacy, highlighting the contexts in which specific AI approaches may offer enhanced diagnostic capabilities. The results aim to inform the integration of AI-driven diagnostic tools in clinical practice, particularly in automated and high-throughput environments where timely, reliable diagnosis is critical. This comparative study addresses an essential gap, establishing guidance for the selection of AI models based on clinical and operational needs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comparative evaluation of radiomics-based and deep learning-based models for disease detection in chest radiography, focusing on COVID-19, lung opacity, and viral pneumonia. It compares the performance of traditional machine learning models (Decision Trees, Gradient Boosting, Random Forests, SVM, MLP) using radiomic features against state-of-the-art deep learning architectures (ConvNeXtXLarge, EfficientNetL, InceptionV3) trained directly on image data. The study assesses diagnostic accuracy and robustness across varying sample sizes. The key findings suggest that deep learning models, particularly InceptionV3 and EfficientNetL, outperform traditional models with larger datasets, while traditional models like SVM and Random Forest offer reliable alternatives with limited data.  The paper highlights the importance of tailoring model selection to available data and computational resources.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Comparison:** The paper provides a valuable direct comparison between a range of radiomics and deep learning models, which is useful for researchers and practitioners choosing appropriate models for their specific needs.
    *   **Focus on Sample Size:** The explicit evaluation of model performance across varying sample sizes is a significant contribution. It addresses a practical concern often faced in medical imaging, where large, labeled datasets may be scarce. This allows for informed decision-making based on data availability.
    *   **Clear Methodology:**  The paper outlines a clear methodology, detailing the dataset used, preprocessing steps, model training procedures, and evaluation metrics. This promotes reproducibility and allows for meaningful comparisons.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings offer practical guidance for selecting AI models based on clinical and operational needs. The conclusion emphasizes tailoring model selection to available data and resources.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Limitations:** While the dataset is sourced from multiple repositories, it is still a composite dataset which may contain inherent biases (e.g., variations in image acquisition protocols, patient populations). The paper acknowledges this limitation but does not explore its potential impact in detail. Out-of-distribution generalization tests were not performed, thus limiting the scope.
    *   **Radiomics Approach Simplicity:** The paper acknowledges using a "relatively simple radiomics-based approach." Exploring more advanced radiomics techniques or hybrid models combining radiomics and deep learning could further enhance the analysis. Also, feature selection using only SelectKBest might be too simplistic, and more advanced feature selection or engineering could improve the performance of radiomics-based models.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Model Architectures:** The deep learning models used are well-established architectures (InceptionV3, EfficientNetL, ConvNeXtXLarge).  While the application and comparison are valuable, the paper doesn't introduce any novel model architectures or training techniques.
    *   **Lack of Clinical Validation:** The paper focuses primarily on computational performance. A more significant contribution would involve some level of clinical validation or evaluation with practicing radiologists to assess the real-world impact and usability of the different models.
    *   **Limited discussion of Class Imbalance**. Although, stratified sampling was employed, there still might be residual effects of class imbalance, so further balancing techniques (e.g. SMOTE) should have been explored.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's significance lies in its pragmatic approach to model selection in medical image analysis. It provides a useful guide for researchers and clinicians who need to choose between radiomics and deep learning approaches, particularly when data is limited. However, the incremental nature of the improvements, coupled with the limited exploration of dataset biases and radiomics techniques, slightly diminishes the overall impact. Also, the performance metrics are standard, and no statistical significance was tested in the paper, thus undermining its claims.

*   **Novelty:**

    The direct comparison between a range of models and the explicit focus on the impact of sample size do add some novelty. The approach of comparing these methods is not entirely novel.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

The paper provides a valuable and well-executed comparison of radiomics and deep learning models for chest radiograph analysis. Its strength is the practical advice it offers on model selection based on data availability. However, the limitations related to dataset biases, the simplicity of the radiomics approach, lack of statistical significance testing, limited novelty of model architecture, and the absence of any clinical validation prevent it from achieving a higher score. Its contribution is largely incremental, consolidating existing knowledge and providing practical guidance rather than introducing groundbreaking innovations.

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### DMM: Building a Versatile Image Generation Model via Distillation-Based Model Merging
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12364v1)
- **Authors**: Tianhui Song, Weixin Feng, Shuai Wang, Xubin Li, Tiezheng Ge, Bo Zheng, Limin Wang
- **Abstract**: The success of text-to-image (T2I) generation models has spurred a proliferation of numerous model checkpoints fine-tuned from the same base model on various specialized datasets. This overwhelming specialized model production introduces new challenges for high parameter redundancy and huge storage cost, thereby necessitating the development of effective methods to consolidate and unify the capabilities of diverse powerful models into a single one. A common practice in model merging adopts static linear interpolation in the parameter space to achieve the goal of style mixing. However, it neglects the features of T2I generation task that numerous distinct models cover sundry styles which may lead to incompatibility and confusion in the merged model. To address this issue, we introduce a style-promptable image generation pipeline which can accurately generate arbitrary-style images under the control of style vectors. Based on this design, we propose the score distillation based model merging paradigm (DMM), compressing multiple models into a single versatile T2I model. Moreover, we rethink and reformulate the model merging task in the context of T2I generation, by presenting new merging goals and evaluation protocols. Our experiments demonstrate that DMM can compactly reorganize the knowledge from multiple teacher models and achieve controllable arbitrary-style generation.
- **Summary**: **Summary:** The paper presents a novel approach called DMM (Distillation-Based Model Merging) aimed at addressing the challenges posed by the proliferation of diverse text-to-image (T2I) generation models. These models, typically fine-tuned on specialized datasets, result in redundancy and increased storage costs. Traditional model merging techniques, which rely on static linear interpolation, fail to harness the distinct styles and features of various models effectively. The authors propose a style-promptable image generation pipeline that utilizes style vectors to control arbitrary-style image outputs. The DMM paradigm compresses multiple teacher models into a unified T2I model while rethinking the merging task with new goals and evaluation protocols. Experiments reveal that DMM can effectively consolidate knowledge from various models and produce controllable images across different styles. **Critical Evaluation:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of image generation by addressing the pressing issues of model redundancy and storage costs, which are critical in practical applications. The introduction of a style-promptable approach is particularly noteworthy, as it enhances the flexibility and versatility of T2I models, allowing users to generate images in varied styles, which has substantial implications for creative applications, content generation, and user-centered design. However, the paper could be strengthened by a more comprehensive evaluation of the proposed merging strategy against existing methods. While the authors present promising experimental results, the lack of a detailed comparative analysis limits the understanding of DMM's effectiveness relative to other contemporary techniques in the literature. Furthermore, the scalability of the approach to future models and the practicality of real-world applications could be further elaborated. Overall, the paper is innovative and presents a well-defined methodology that potentially advances the state of T2I generation models. The ideas introduced can influence both academic research and industrial applications in the field. Therefore, considering its contribution, applicability, and the need for further analysis, the paper deserves a **high score**. Score: 8
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Themisto: Jupyter-Based Runtime Benchmark
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12365v1)
- **Authors**: Konstantin Grotov, Sergey Titov
- **Abstract**: In this work, we present a benchmark that consists of Jupyter notebooks development trajectories and allows measuring how large language models (LLMs) can leverage runtime information for predicting code output and code generation. We demonstrate that the current generation of LLMs performs poorly on these tasks and argue that there exists a significantly understudied domain in the development of code-based models, which involves incorporating the runtime context.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a benchmark called THEMISTO for evaluating the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to utilize runtime information in the context of Jupyter notebook development. The benchmark consists of Jupyter notebook development trajectories, capturing the sequence of cell executions and their resulting outputs. The authors propose two tasks: next cell prediction (predicting the code of the subsequent cell) and cell output prediction (predicting the output of a given cell).  The paper evaluates several popular LLMs on these tasks, demonstrating that current models struggle to effectively leverage runtime information. The benchmark dataset is made publicly available on Zenodo.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in the benchmark itself. While prior work has explored code completion and generation, the focus on leveraging dynamic runtime information within Jupyter notebook development trajectories is a relatively under-explored area. Specifically, creating a dataset of real user interaction within a REPL environment that includes code and runtime context is valuable. The combination of code generation and output prediction tasks also offers a unique perspective. However, the core idea of using runtime information isn't entirely novel; previous research explored this direction. Therefore, the novelty is moderate, being primarily in the specific benchmark and its application.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights an important limitation of current LLMs in code-related tasks: their inability to effectively utilize runtime context. This limitation has practical implications for code completion, debugging, and interactive development. By providing a benchmark to measure this capability, the authors aim to stimulate research in this area. The significance is amplified by making the dataset publicly available, facilitating further investigation. However, the authors admit that the dataset is limited to two tasks from only 20 participants with a small subset used for this paper, so it doesn't have the variability necessary to represent a substantial contribution to the field. The dataset is a subsample of a larger dataset from their group, and is limited by those earlier constraints.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The benchmark addresses a gap in current evaluation practices for code-based LLMs.
    *   The inclusion of Jupyter notebook trajectories provides a realistic development context.
    *   The dataset is made publicly available.
    *   The tasks of next cell prediction and output prediction are relevant to practical development scenarios.
    *   Provides baseline results using strong open and closed sourced LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The dataset is relatively small and based on a limited number of tasks and participants, reducing the generalizability of the findings. This is a crucial point acknowledged by the authors.
    *   The evaluation focuses on exact match and ROUGE/ChrF metrics, which may not fully capture the nuances of code generation and understanding. Other metrics, such as those that explicitly evaluate the semantic correctness of generated code or the usefulness of predicted outputs, could be considered.
    *   The runtime information aspect is not fully explored. The paper mentions the inclusion of runtime variables and memory usage, but the baseline evaluation doesn't deeply analyze how these specific features impact the performance of LLMs. A more granular analysis of the impact of different types of runtime information would strengthen the paper.
    *   Limited baseline exploration: the baseline results do not deeply analyze model architectures, or alternative prompting strategies that could be used.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on code-based LLMs by encouraging the development of models that can effectively utilize runtime context. The benchmark can serve as a valuable tool for comparing and evaluating different approaches. However, the limited size and scope of the dataset may restrict its long-term impact. Its primary value is highlighting the need for datasets that are more carefully constructed for runtime analysis.

**Score: 5**

**Justification:** The paper introduces a useful benchmark for evaluating LLMs in a practical coding context. However, the limitations in the dataset's size and diversity, as well as the limited analysis of runtime information, constrain the paper's novelty and overall impact. While the paper identifies an important research direction and makes a contribution through the dataset's release, the benchmark has some considerable issues that need to be addressed before more concrete contributions can be made. The value of the work is therefore limited, as stronger datasets with more variability need to be collected before claims about generalizing code generations can be made.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 5/10

### InstantCharacter: Personalize Any Characters with a Scalable Diffusion Transformer Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12395v1)
- **Authors**: Jiale Tao, Yanbing Zhang, Qixun Wang, Yiji Cheng, Haofan Wang, Xu Bai, Zhengguang Zhou, Ruihuang Li, Linqing Wang, Chunyu Wang, Qin Lin, Qinglin Lu
- **Abstract**: Current learning-based subject customization approaches, predominantly relying on U-Net architectures, suffer from limited generalization ability and compromised image quality. Meanwhile, optimization-based methods require subject-specific fine-tuning, which inevitably degrades textual controllability. To address these challenges, we propose InstantCharacter, a scalable framework for character customization built upon a foundation diffusion transformer. InstantCharacter demonstrates three fundamental advantages: first, it achieves open-domain personalization across diverse character appearances, poses, and styles while maintaining high-fidelity results. Second, the framework introduces a scalable adapter with stacked transformer encoders, which effectively processes open-domain character features and seamlessly interacts with the latent space of modern diffusion transformers. Third, to effectively train the framework, we construct a large-scale character dataset containing 10-million-level samples. The dataset is systematically organized into paired (multi-view character) and unpaired (text-image combinations) subsets. This dual-data structure enables simultaneous optimization of identity consistency and textual editability through distinct learning pathways. Qualitative experiments demonstrate the advanced capabilities of InstantCharacter in generating high-fidelity, text-controllable, and character-consistent images, setting a new benchmark for character-driven image generation. Our source code is available at https://github.com/Tencent/InstantCharacter.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "InstantCharacter," a novel diffusion transformer framework for personalized character image generation. It aims to address limitations in existing methods, particularly those based on U-Net architectures, which struggle with generalization and image quality, and optimization-based methods, which suffer from degraded textual controllability and computational cost. InstantCharacter features a scalable adapter built upon a foundation diffusion transformer (DiT) to achieve open-domain personalization across diverse character appearances, poses, and styles, while maintaining high-fidelity results. A key component is a scalable adapter with stacked transformer encoders that process character features and interacts seamlessly with the DiT's latent space. To facilitate training, the authors construct a large-scale character dataset containing multi-view character images and unpaired text-image combinations. A three-stage training strategy optimizes identity consistency, textual editability, and image fidelity.  The framework is implemented based on the FLUX1.0-dev model and demonstrates superior performance in generating high-fidelity, text-controllable, and character-consistent images, establishing a new benchmark for character-driven image generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the following aspects:
    *   **DiT-based Character Personalization:**  It is one of the first frameworks to leverage the power of Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) specifically for personalized character generation. Previous methods primarily relied on U-Net architectures. Shifting to DiTs is a significant architectural change that offers potential improvements in image quality and generative capacity.
    *   **Scalable Adapter Design:** The proposed adapter architecture is designed to effectively integrate multi-stage character features within the DiT latent space. The stacked transformer encoders and the hierarchical integration strategy offer a more sophisticated approach than simpler cross-attention mechanisms used in prior adapter-based methods. This design may contribute to better generalization and fidelity.
    *   **Large-Scale Dataset and Training Strategy:** The construction of a large, versatile character dataset containing both paired and unpaired data, along with the tailored three-stage training strategy, is also a significant contribution. This systematic approach to data and training likely addresses challenges associated with maintaining identity consistency and textual control.
    *   **Open-domain character customization:** The method appears to successfully customize open-domain characters with varying appearances, poses, and styles, which sets it apart from previous approaches that focused on narrower domains.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is multi-fold:

    *   **Improved Image Quality and Controllability:** The qualitative results show a marked improvement in image quality, character consistency, and text controllability compared to existing methods. If these findings are validated by quantitative evaluation, InstantCharacter could represent a significant step forward in character-driven image generation.
    *   **Framework for Future Research:** The paper provides a valuable framework for future research in this area. The DiT-based architecture, scalable adapter design, and training strategy can serve as a foundation for further exploration and improvement.
    *   **Potential Applications:** The ability to generate high-quality, character-consistent, and text-controllable images has numerous applications in creative industries, including storytelling, comic creation, game development, e-commerce, and advertising.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The DiT adoption brings in the benefits of Transformer-based architectures, like better long-range dependencies.
    *   The detailed description of the adapter design and training strategy enables reproducibility and future research.
    *   The paper's claim of superior generalizability is backed by qualitative results showing diverse character appearances, poses, and styles.
    *   The curated dataset will likely facilitate future research.
    *   Clear explanation of the method's motivations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on FLUX1.0-dev:** The framework is built upon the FLUX1.0-dev model, meaning that its success is tied to this particular DiT implementation. A more thorough investigation of its performance with different DiT architectures would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited Quantitative Evaluation:** While the qualitative results are compelling, the lack of extensive quantitative evaluation, especially user studies, makes it challenging to objectively assess the true impact of InstantCharacter. Metrics related to identity consistency, textual accuracy, and image quality (e.g., FID, CLIP score, identity metrics) should be reported.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Although the paper aims for inference-time efficiency by using an adapter, the computational cost of training InstantCharacter on a 10-million-level dataset is likely significant. This should be discussed, and comparisons should be made to the training costs of other methods.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** With such a large dataset, there is also the potential for biases to be learned, which could affect the generated images. Analysis of potential biases should be addressed.
    *   The reference of code is unavailable at this point which doesn't allow replication of results.

*   **Impact:**
    *   This research can inspire further explorations of DiT-based frameworks for personalized image generation.
    *   The work addresses the challenges of achieving high-fidelity, character consistency, and textual control in personalized image generation.
    *   The paper has the potential to influence the development of future tools and technologies in the creative industries.

**Overall:**
The paper presents a compelling approach to personalized character image generation using DiTs, but could benefit from more thorough quantitative evaluation and discussion of limitations.
Score: 7.5
Justification: The score reflects the novelty and potential impact of the work, but also acknowledges the need for more robust quantitative validation and more information about training costs, limitations, and potential biases in data and the DiT model. The design choices are well-explained. A score above 8 would require a more comprehensive evaluation section that included both quantitative metrics and qualitative user studies.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Human-AI Comparative Analysis of Prompt Sensitivity in LLM-Based Relevance Judgment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12408v1)
- **Authors**: Negar Arabzadeh, Charles L. A . Clarke
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used to automate relevance judgments for information retrieval (IR) tasks, often demonstrating agreement with human labels that approaches inter-human agreement. To assess the robustness and reliability of LLM-based relevance judgments, we systematically investigate impact of prompt sensitivity on the task. We collected prompts for relevance assessment from 15 human experts and 15 LLMs across three tasks~ -- ~binary, graded, and pairwise~ -- ~yielding 90 prompts in total. After filtering out unusable prompts from three humans and three LLMs, we employed the remaining 72 prompts with three different LLMs as judges to label document/query pairs from two TREC Deep Learning Datasets (2020 and 2021). We compare LLM-generated labels with TREC official human labels using Cohen's $\kappa$ and pairwise agreement measures. In addition to investigating the impact of prompt variations on agreement with human labels, we compare human- and LLM-generated prompts and analyze differences among different LLMs as judges. We also compare human- and LLM-generated prompts with the standard UMBRELA prompt used for relevance assessment by Bing and TREC 2024 Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) Track. To support future research in LLM-based evaluation, we release all data and prompts at https://github.com/Narabzad/prompt-sensitivity-relevance-judgements/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the prompt sensitivity of Large Language Models (LLMs) when used for relevance judgment in information retrieval. The authors collected prompts from both human experts and LLMs themselves, and evaluated the performance of different LLMs (GPT-40, LLAMA 3.2, and Mistral 7B) using these prompts on TREC Deep Learning datasets (DL 2020 and DL 2021). They compare the LLM-generated judgments with human labels, analyzing the impact of different prompts on binary, graded, and pairwise relevance assessment tasks. The study examines the effectiveness of LLMs as prompt generators, the robustness of prompts across different LLMs, and model-specific prompt sensitivity. The authors find significant prompt-based variance, especially for graded relevance judgments. They also observe that LLM-generated prompts often yield higher agreement with human annotations and that GPT-40 exhibits greater consistency across prompts than LLAMA 3.2 and Mistral 7B.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The study is novel in its systematic investigation of prompt sensitivity in LLM-based relevance judgment across multiple tasks (binary, graded, and pairwise), different LLMs as judges, and both human-generated and LLM-generated prompts. While previous studies have examined prompt engineering for LLMs in various contexts, this paper provides a comprehensive comparative analysis specifically focused on relevance assessment in IR, using real-world TREC datasets. The comparison of human-generated prompts against LLM-generated prompts is a welcome addition to this burgeoning area.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant for the IR community, as LLMs are increasingly being adopted for relevance assessment. Understanding the impact of prompt variations is crucial for ensuring the reliability and robustness of LLM-based evaluation. The paper highlights the importance of careful prompt design and model selection when using LLMs for this task, particularly for graded relevance assessments which show higher prompt-based variance. The discovery that LLMs generated prompts do as well or better is also quite interesting and could save a lot of human effort. Furthermore, the public release of the dataset promotes further study in this important area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Design:** The study employs a well-designed experimental setup, covering multiple tasks, models, and prompt sources.
    *   **Use of Standard Datasets:** The use of TREC Deep Learning datasets allows for comparison with existing research and provides a realistic evaluation scenario.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the results, including statistical measures of agreement and comparisons across different models and prompts.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors make their data and prompts publicly available, promoting reproducibility and further research.
    *   **Evaluation of UMBRELA prompt** A strength of this paper is comparing other prompts to the UMBRELA prompt. This is a standard and robust prompt developed by a large organization (Microsoft/Bing) that has been show to have a high degree of agreement. The fact that it is integrated into the TREC 2024 RAG evaluation is also a testament to its significance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Number of LLMs as Judges:** While three LLMs were used as judges, the conclusions would be stronger with a broader selection of models.
    *   **Sampling Strategy:** Although the paper acknowledges limitations due to computational constraints, the random sampling strategy for GPT-40's evaluation might introduce some bias. A more systematic sampling approach, if feasible, could improve the robustness of the results.
    *   **Human Participant Pool:** Restricting human participants primarily to academia NLP/IR labs and those with previous publications may lead to some homogeneity that does not represent the full range of prompt generation that happens.
    *   **Temperature setting of 0:** This makes the models deterministic, which might reduce some of the value of testing multiple different prompts.
