- **Score**: 7/10

### Generalization through variance: how noise shapes inductive biases in diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12532v1)
- **Authors**: John J. Vastola
- **Abstract**: How diffusion models generalize beyond their training set is not known, and is somewhat mysterious given two facts: the optimum of the denoising score matching (DSM) objective usually used to train diffusion models is the score function of the training distribution; and the networks usually used to learn the score function are expressive enough to learn this score to high accuracy. We claim that a certain feature of the DSM objective -- the fact that its target is not the training distribution's score, but a noisy quantity only equal to it in expectation -- strongly impacts whether and to what extent diffusion models generalize. In this paper, we develop a mathematical theory that partly explains this 'generalization through variance' phenomenon. Our theoretical analysis exploits a physics-inspired path integral approach to compute the distributions typically learned by a few paradigmatic under- and overparameterized diffusion models. We find that the distributions diffusion models effectively learn to sample from resemble their training distributions, but with 'gaps' filled in, and that this inductive bias is due to the covariance structure of the noisy target used during training. We also characterize how this inductive bias interacts with feature-related inductive biases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how diffusion models generalize beyond their training data. It argues that the "denoising score matching" (DSM) objective, specifically the inherent noise in the target score function (the 'proxy score'), significantly influences generalization.  The authors develop a mathematical theory based on the Martin-Siggia-Rose (MSR) path integral formalism to analyze distributions learned by diffusion models.  They find that diffusion models learn distributions that resemble the training data but fill in "gaps" due to the covariance structure of the noisy target used during training. The paper also analyzes how this "generalization through variance" interacts with feature-related inductive biases and explores these interactions in linear models and infinite-width neural networks. The paper shows how diffusion models generalize even when trained with interleaved training and sampling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core argument – that the variance of the proxy score in the DSM objective is a crucial driver of generalization in diffusion models – is a relatively novel perspective. While score matching and its properties are well-studied, focusing on the *variance* induced by using the noisy target and how it shapes the learned distribution represents a fresh contribution. The application of the MSR path integral formalism to analyzing diffusion models is also a novel methodological component. However, empirical studies have demonstrated many of the concepts studied in the paper, leaving some uncertainty to the extent to which the paper's conclusions are surprising.

*   **Significance:** Understanding generalization in diffusion models is a central problem. The paper aims at a mechanistic explanation of a phenomenon that is often treated purely empirically.  If the "generalization through variance" framework is well-supported by future work, it could provide a principled way to design better diffusion models, understand their limitations, and potentially improve their robustness. However, at the current state, the work should be considered speculative.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Formalism:** The use of the MSR path integral provides a sophisticated mathematical tool for analyzing diffusion model behavior.
    *   **Clear Hypothesis:** The paper clearly articulates its central hypothesis and explores its implications.
    *   **Theoretical Depth:** The analysis goes beyond high-level arguments and delves into specific model classes (linear models, NTK networks) to illustrate the theory.
    *   **Connection to Empirical Observations:** The paper attempts to link its theoretical findings to existing empirical observations about diffusion models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Idealized Setting:** The theoretical analysis relies on simplified assumptions about the model architecture, training process (e.g., full-batch gradient descent), and access to an infinite amount of data. The extent to which the results generalize to real-world diffusion models is not thoroughly demonstrated. The paper acknowledges these limitations.
    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** While the paper cites empirical work, it does not include substantial new empirical validation of its specific theoretical predictions. The included figures are toy datasets used to illustrate the claims of the paper, and not real datasets used in diffusion modelling.
    *   **Technical Difficulty:** The paper is highly technical and requires a strong background in probability theory, statistical physics, and machine learning. This will limit its accessibility to a broader audience.
    *   **Limited Practical Impact:** Despite the theoretical insights, the practical implications of the "generalization through variance" framework are not fully clear. How can one concretely leverage this understanding to build better diffusion models?

*   **Potential Influence:** If the theoretical claims stand the test of time and future empirical studies support its claims, and can be turned into concrete design principles, the paper could influence future research in diffusion modeling.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel perspective and theoretical framework for understanding generalization in diffusion models, which are two central features that make the paper very interesting. However, its reliance on strong assumptions, and missing empirical backing, weakens its overall impact. The technical complexity also limits its broad accessibility. A score of 6.5 reflects a solid contribution with significant potential but substantial gaps in validation and immediate practical relevance. Further research will be needed to determine the ultimate significance of this work.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Knowledge Acquisition on Mass-shooting Events via LLMs for AI-Driven Justice
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12545v1)
- **Authors**: Benign John Ihugba, Afsana Nasrin, Ling Wu, Lin Li, Lijun Qian, Xishuang Dong
- **Abstract**: Mass-shooting events pose a significant challenge to public safety, generating large volumes of unstructured textual data that hinder effective investigations and the formulation of public policy. Despite the urgency, few prior studies have effectively automated the extraction of key information from these events to support legal and investigative efforts. This paper presented the first dataset designed for knowledge acquisition on mass-shooting events through the application of named entity recognition (NER) techniques. It focuses on identifying key entities such as offenders, victims, locations, and criminal instruments, that are vital for legal and investigative purposes. The NER process is powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) using few-shot prompting, facilitating the efficient extraction and organization of critical information from diverse sources, including news articles, police reports, and social media. Experimental results on real-world mass-shooting corpora demonstrate that GPT-4o is the most effective model for mass-shooting NER, achieving the highest Micro Precision, Micro Recall, and Micro F1-scores. Meanwhile, o1-mini delivers competitive performance, making it a resource-efficient alternative for less complex NER tasks. It is also observed that increasing the shot count enhances the performance of all models, but the gains are more substantial for GPT-4o and o1-mini, highlighting their superior adaptability to few-shot learning scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of extracting key information from unstructured text data related to mass-shooting events to support AI-driven justice.  It introduces a new dataset and leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) with few-shot prompting for named entity recognition (NER). The study focuses on identifying entities like offenders, victims, locations, and criminal instruments.  The authors experiment with GPT-3.5, GPT-4o, and o1-mini, finding that GPT-4o achieves the best performance across various NER metrics. The paper also shows that increasing the number of "shots" in few-shot learning improves performance, especially for GPT-4o and o1-mini.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to present the first dataset for knowledge acquisition on mass shooting events specifically designed for NER using LLMs. This claim, if true, constitutes a significant contribution. However, the degree of novelty hinges on the uniqueness of the annotation schema and the specific choice of LLMs used. While LLMs are increasingly applied to various NLP tasks, the authors present a domain-specific dataset and NER task for the justice domain related to mass shooting events, which is important and is a unique area of focus. The systematic evaluation and examination of LLMs on this specific task also add to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** Mass shootings are a critical societal problem, and AI-driven tools could potentially aid in investigation, policy-making, and prevention. The paper's effort to structure and extract information from event reports can be considered beneficial for creating knowledge graphs that support such applications. LLM performance in such critical tasks is essential in real-world applications. The research contributes to the broader goal of applying AI to improve public safety and justice.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Focus on a Relevant Problem:** Addresses a pressing issue with real-world implications.
    *   **Dataset Creation:** The creation and annotation of a new dataset are valuable. However, there is a limited set of data that only contains 153 entries.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** A thorough performance comparison across multiple LLMs and with different shot counts in few-shot learning. The use of Micro Precision, Micro Recall, and Micro F1-scores provides a holistic view of the performance.
    *   **Resource-Efficiency Analysis:** Comparing GPT-4o and ol-mini is insightful, showing how to balance performance and computational cost, which is important for real-world deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Size:** The dataset size of 153 events could be a limitation. More comprehensive datasets would be more helpful to generalize results.
    *   **LLM Selection:** The focus is only on OpenAI models. The evaluation should include models from other vendors, such as Google or Meta.
    *   **Task Complexity:** The task of extracting these entities is not particularly complex.
    *   **Annotation Process:** While the paper mentions expert supervision and iterative annotation rounds, more detailed information on inter-annotator agreement and the process used for resolving disagreements would improve the reliability of the results.
    *   **Error Analysis:** A detailed error analysis explaining which entities are difficult to recognize and the source of errors would greatly improve the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The dataset and findings could influence future research in AI-driven justice, particularly in the application of LLMs to analyzing mass-shooting events and similar critical incidents. The work contributes to the growing literature on few-shot learning and prompt engineering for NER in specialized domains.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is moderately novel in its application of LLMs to knowledge acquisition from mass-shooting events and offers a practical dataset. While the chosen task isn't inherently groundbreaking and the scope of the dataset is limited, the insights from comparing different LLMs and few-shot learning strategies contribute to the field.
The authors present a domain-specific dataset and NER task for the justice domain related to mass shooting events, which is important and is a unique area of focus. The systematic evaluation and examination of LLMs on this specific task also add to the novelty.
The analysis of resource efficiency, comparing GPT-4o with the smaller ol-mini, adds a practical dimension to the research. Further work to include diverse LLMs and provide a comprehensive study in order to better contribute to the mass shooting domain.

### ELAB: Extensive LLM Alignment Benchmark in Persian Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12553v1)
- **Authors**: Zahra Pourbahman, Fatemeh Rajabi, Mohammadhossein Sadeghi, Omid Ghahroodi, Somaye Bakhshaei, Arash Amini, Reza Kazemi, Mahdieh Soleymani Baghshah
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation framework for aligning Persian Large Language Models (LLMs) with critical ethical dimensions, including safety, fairness, and social norms. It addresses the gaps in existing LLM evaluation frameworks by adapting them to Persian linguistic and cultural contexts. This benchmark creates three types of Persian-language benchmarks: (i) translated data, (ii) new data generated synthetically, and (iii) new naturally collected data. We translate Anthropic Red Teaming data, AdvBench, HarmBench, and DecodingTrust into Persian. Furthermore, we create ProhibiBench-fa, SafeBench-fa, FairBench-fa, and SocialBench-fa as new datasets to address harmful and prohibited content in indigenous culture. Moreover, we collect extensive dataset as GuardBench-fa to consider Persian cultural norms. By combining these datasets, our work establishes a unified framework for evaluating Persian LLMs, offering a new approach to culturally grounded alignment evaluation. A systematic evaluation of Persian LLMs is performed across the three alignment aspects: safety (avoiding harmful content), fairness (mitigating biases), and social norms (adhering to culturally accepted behaviors). We present a publicly available leaderboard that benchmarks Persian LLMs with respect to safety, fairness, and social norms at: https://huggingface.co/spaces/MCILAB/LLM_Alignment_Evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ELAB, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating the alignment of Persian Large Language Models (LLMs). Recognizing the limitations of existing, primarily English-focused, alignment frameworks, ELAB addresses the specific linguistic and cultural nuances of Persian. It comprises three types of datasets: translated versions of established benchmarks (Anthropic, AdvBench, HarmBench, DecodingTrust), synthetically generated Persian datasets (ProhibiBench-fa, SafeBench-fa, FairBench-fa, SocialBench-fa), and a naturally collected Persian dataset (GuardBench-fa).  These datasets are designed to evaluate safety, fairness, and adherence to social norms within the Persian cultural context. The paper presents a unified framework for evaluation, including a publicly available leaderboard, and reports initial evaluation results on several Persian LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses an Important Gap:** The paper tackles a critical and largely unaddressed problem: the evaluation of LLM alignment in non-English languages, specifically Persian. This is essential for ensuring that LLMs are not only functional but also ethical and culturally appropriate in diverse contexts.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset Construction:**  The combination of translated, synthetically generated, and naturally collected data is a strong point. It leverages existing resources while also creating datasets that are specifically tailored to the Persian language and culture. The careful back-translation and cultural coherence checks add rigor.
    *   **Culturally Grounded Approach:**  The paper explicitly considers cultural nuances like 'taarof' and 'aberoo,' which are vital for avoiding stereotypes and biases in Persian LLMs. The introduction of datasets like GuardBench-fa, which focus on Persian cultural norms, is a significant contribution.
    *   **Unified Framework and Leaderboard:**  The creation of a unified evaluation framework and a public leaderboard promotes transparency and facilitates cross-model comparisons, fostering further research and development in the field.
    *   **Detailed Methodological Approach:** The paper provides an in-depth explanation of dataset generation methodologies which enable a systematic assessment of LLMs’ performances

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Translation:** While the translation of existing benchmarks is necessary, it doesn't represent significant conceptual novelty. The real innovation lies in the creation of the new, Persian-specific datasets.
    *   **LLM-as-a-Judge Limitations:** Relying on LLM-as-a-Judge can introduce biases, even when using a model as capable as GPT-40 mini. Human evaluation, though more costly, would provide a more reliable ground truth. While the paper acknowledges limitations to LLM-as-a-Judge, the biases it may bring to the leaderboard results need to be discussed more thoroughly.
    *   **Model selection:** A thorough justification of the selected models has to be added, for example explaining the reasons that the selected models are the most appropriate to benchmark and use to evaluate the alignment of Persian LLMs

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a foundational resource for researchers and developers working on Persian LLMs. It enables a more nuanced and culturally sensitive evaluation of these models, promoting the development of AI systems that are better aligned with human values in the Persian-speaking world.
    *   The methodology and datasets introduced in this paper can serve as a template for developing similar alignment benchmarks in other low-resource languages and cultural contexts.
    *  The detailed documentation regarding dataset construction and evaluation paves the way for future research and contribution to the field.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

ELAB represents a significant and practical contribution to the field of multilingual LLM alignment. The creation of Persian-specific datasets that consider cultural nuances is particularly valuable and provides a concrete step towards addressing the limitations of existing, primarily English-focused, frameworks. The comprehensiveness of the framework with translated, synthetic, and collected data is another strong suit. However, the reliance on translation for a portion of the benchmark, and the limitations associated with LLM-as-a-Judge, slightly reduce the overall novelty and robustness of the paper. The significance lies in the opening of a previously underexplored research area, creating benchmarks that could be built upon for the future.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmarking LLM-based Relevance Judgment Methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12558v1)
- **Authors**: Negar Arabzadeh, Charles L. A. Clarke
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in both academic and industry settings to automate the evaluation of information seeking systems, particularly by generating graded relevance judgments. Previous work on LLM-based relevance assessment has primarily focused on replicating graded human relevance judgments through various prompting strategies. However, there has been limited exploration of alternative assessment methods or comprehensive comparative studies. In this paper, we systematically compare multiple LLM-based relevance assessment methods, including binary relevance judgments, graded relevance assessments, pairwise preference-based methods, and two nugget-based evaluation methods~--~document-agnostic and document-dependent. In addition to a traditional comparison based on system rankings using Kendall correlations, we also examine how well LLM judgments align with human preferences, as inferred from relevance grades. We conduct extensive experiments on datasets from three TREC Deep Learning tracks 2019, 2020 and 2021 as well as the ANTIQUE dataset, which focuses on non-factoid open-domain question answering. As part of our data release, we include relevance judgments generated by both an open-source (Llama3.2b) and a commercial (gpt-4o) model. Our goal is to \textit{reproduce} various LLM-based relevance judgment methods to provide a comprehensive comparison. All code, data, and resources are publicly available in our GitHub Repository at https://github.com/Narabzad/llm-relevance-judgement-comparison.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the increasing use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in automating relevance judgment for information retrieval (IR) evaluation. It benchmarks various LLM-based relevance assessment methods, including binary relevance, graded relevance, pairwise preferences, and two nugget-based methods. The study systematically compares these methods across several datasets (TREC Deep Learning and ANTIQUE) and two LLMs (GPT-40 and Llama 3.2B). The comparison uses two key factors: alignment with human labels (assessing if the ordering reflects human judgments) and agreement with system rankings (consistency with rankings derived from human labels). The authors contribute a comprehensive resource of LLM-generated relevance judgments and offer a methodology for evaluating and comparing diverse LLM-based assessment methods, emphasizing reproducibility and minimizing bias.

**Novelty and Significance:**

Here's a critical evaluation of the paper's novelty and significance, focusing on both strengths and weaknesses:

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Comparison:** The paper's primary strength is its systematic and comprehensive comparison of various LLM-based relevance assessment methods. Unlike prior work that often focuses on replicating graded human relevance judgments, this paper explores alternative methods and provides a comparative analysis. It goes beyond simply reporting Kendall's Tau correlation and also examines alignment with human preferences.
    *   **Methodological Contribution:** The methodology presented for comparing LLM-based relevance judgments is a significant contribution. The paper clearly defines and operationalizes two key factors: alignment with human labels and agreement with system rankings. This provides a framework for future research in this area.
    *   **Resource Contribution:** The release of a large dataset of LLM-generated relevance judgments across multiple methods, models, and datasets is invaluable. This resource serves as a baseline for future research and allows for reproducible experimentation.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The commitment to reproducibility is evident in the authors' reliance on established tools and prompts, as well as the public availability of code, data, and resources.
    *   **Focus on Alternative Methods:** The inclusion of nugget-based and pairwise preference methods addresses a gap in the literature by expanding the evaluation beyond graded relevance assessments.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper is comprehensive, the individual LLM-based methods evaluated are not entirely novel, and the paper relies heavily on established techniques for evaluation (Kendall's Tau, NDCG variations). The core novelty lies in the *comparison* and *systematic analysis*.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The results indicate that the performance of some methods varies across datasets. While this is an interesting finding, the paper doesn't fully explain *why* this variation occurs. This limits the practical guidance for practitioners on selecting appropriate methods for specific types of datasets or tasks.
    *   **Limited Analysis of LLM Differences:** While the paper mentions using both GPT-40 and Llama 3.2B, the main analysis focuses on the results from GPT-40. The comparison between the two models is not highlighted within the paper, which diminishes the findings given the rapidly evolving LLM landscape.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Insights:** Although the paper presents correlations and compatibility metrics, it could benefit from providing more in-depth insights.  For example, the observation that nugget-based methods perform poorly on the ANTIQUE dataset could have been explored further by analyzing the kinds of nuggets generated and their relevance to the non-factoid queries.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses a critical need in the IR community: how to effectively and reliably use LLMs for evaluation.
    *   The findings have implications for how researchers and practitioners approach automated relevance assessment.
    *   The resource provided will likely be widely used and cited.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable and timely contribution to the field of information retrieval. It provides a comprehensive, reproducible benchmark for comparing LLM-based relevance assessment methods and offers a useful methodology for future research.  While the individual methods are not new, the systematic comparative analysis and the resource contribution are significant. However, the limitations in deeper analysis and the limited exploration of differences between LLMs keeps it from achieving a truly exceptional rating.

### CDF-RAG: Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12560v1)
- **Authors**: Elahe Khatibi, Ziyu Wang, Amir M. Rahmani
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has significantly enhanced large language models (LLMs) in knowledge-intensive tasks by incorporating external knowledge retrieval. However, existing RAG frameworks primarily rely on semantic similarity and correlation-driven retrieval, limiting their ability to distinguish true causal relationships from spurious associations. This results in responses that may be factually grounded but fail to establish cause-and-effect mechanisms, leading to incomplete or misleading insights. To address this issue, we introduce Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation (CDF-RAG), a framework designed to improve causal consistency, factual accuracy, and explainability in generative reasoning. CDF-RAG iteratively refines queries, retrieves structured causal graphs, and enables multi-hop causal reasoning across interconnected knowledge sources. Additionally, it validates responses against causal pathways, ensuring logically coherent and factually grounded outputs. We evaluate CDF-RAG on four diverse datasets, demonstrating its ability to improve response accuracy and causal correctness over existing RAG-based methods. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/ elakhatibi/CDF-RAG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CDF-RAG (Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a framework designed to improve causal consistency, factual accuracy, and explainability in generative reasoning tasks using large language models (LLMs). CDF-RAG addresses the limitations of existing RAG systems, which often rely on semantic similarity-based retrieval and struggle to distinguish true causal relationships from spurious correlations.  The framework iteratively refines queries using reinforcement learning, retrieves structured causal graphs via a dual-path approach (semantic and causal), and validates responses against causal pathways. The authors evaluate CDF-RAG on four diverse question-answering datasets (CosmosQA, MedQA, MedMCQA, AdversarialQA), demonstrating improved accuracy and causal correctness over existing RAG methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of several components into a unified framework to address causal reasoning in RAG.  While individual components like query refinement using RL, causal graph retrieval, and hallucination detection are not entirely new, their combination and orchestration within CDF-RAG to specifically target causal understanding is a significant contribution. The dual-path retrieval strategy combining semantic search with causal graph traversal is also a notable feature.
*   **Significance:**  The paper tackles a crucial problem in applying LLMs to knowledge-intensive tasks: the potential for generating factually grounded but causally flawed or incomplete responses. The proposed framework has the potential to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLM-based systems in domains where causal reasoning is critical, such as medical decision-making or scientific research. The comprehensive experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach across different datasets and LLM backbones.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive framework:** CDF-RAG offers a well-defined and modular architecture that addresses multiple aspects of causal reasoning in RAG.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of CDF-RAG on diverse datasets, demonstrating consistent improvements over strong baselines.
    *   **Detailed ablation study:** The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of each component in the framework.
    *   **Clearly written and well-organized:** The paper is easy to follow and provides sufficient details for replicating the experiments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Causal Graphs:** The framework heavily relies on the availability of structured causal knowledge graphs, which might be a limitation in domains where such resources are scarce or noisy. While the authors describe a method for constructing causal graphs, the quality of the resulting graphs directly affects the performance of CDF-RAG.
    *   **Computational Overhead:**  The use of RL for query refinement and GPT-based validation for hallucination detection can introduce significant computational overhead, potentially limiting the scalability and real-time applicability of the framework.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** While the datasets used are diverse, they still might not cover the full range of complex reasoning scenarios encountered in real-world applications. Further evaluation on more challenging and diverse datasets is needed to assess the generalizability of CDF-RAG.
    *   **GPT-4 Dependency:** The dependency on the GPT-4 API for certain aspects, like causal pair verification in the graph construction, introduces a practical dependency and potential cost factor. An alternative, open-source verification module would strengthen the practicality of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of RAG research, particularly in the development of more robust and trustworthy knowledge-intensive systems. It encourages the community to move beyond simple semantic retrieval and consider incorporating causal reasoning principles into LLM-based systems.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper represents a significant contribution to the field of RAG by introducing a novel and comprehensive framework for improving causal reasoning. The experimental results are compelling, and the ablation study provides valuable insights. However, the reliance on causal graphs, computational overhead, and GPT-4 API dependency are potential limitations that need to be addressed in future research. While the results demonstrate the effectiveness of the current framework, it is not yet fully optimized for broader adoption due to potential issues when scaling and integrating it into real-world applications.

### ZeroSumEval: Scaling LLM Evaluation with Inter-Model Competition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12562v1)
- **Authors**: Haidar Khan, Hisham A. Alyahya, Yazeed Alnumay, M Saiful Bari, Bülent Yener
- **Abstract**: Evaluating the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) has traditionally relied on static benchmark datasets, human assessments, or model-based evaluations - methods that often suffer from overfitting, high costs, and biases. ZeroSumEval is a novel competition-based evaluation protocol that leverages zero-sum games to assess LLMs with dynamic benchmarks that resist saturation. ZeroSumEval encompasses a diverse suite of games, including security challenges (PyJail), classic games (Chess, Liar's Dice, Poker), knowledge tests (MathQuiz), and persuasion challenges (Gandalf, Debate). These games are designed to evaluate a range of AI capabilities such as strategic reasoning, planning, knowledge application, and creativity. Building upon recent studies that highlight the effectiveness of game-based evaluations for LLMs, ZeroSumEval enhances these approaches by providing a standardized and extensible framework. To demonstrate this, we conduct extensive experiments with >7000 simulations across 7 games and 13 models. Our results show that while frontier models from the GPT and Claude families can play common games and answer questions, they struggle to play games that require creating novel and challenging questions. We also observe that models cannot reliably jailbreak each other and fail generally at tasks requiring creativity. We release our code at https://github.com/facebookresearch/ZeroSumEval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ZEROSUMEVAL paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZeroSumEval, a novel framework for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) based on zero-sum games.  Instead of relying on static datasets or human evaluations (which can be costly, biased, and prone to overfitting), ZeroSumEval employs competitive gameplay across a diverse suite of games like Chess, Poker, Liar's Dice, MathQuiz, PyJail, Gandalf and Debate. These games are designed to test various AI capabilities such as strategic reasoning, knowledge application, planning, and creativity. The framework aims to address limitations of traditional benchmarks by providing a standardized, extensible, and dynamic evaluation environment that is more resistant to saturation. The authors conduct experiments with a wide range of models and analyze their performance in these games. They show that while advanced models perform well in standard games and question answering, they struggle with tasks requiring creating novel challenges, creative problem-solving (jailbreaking), and creating valid questions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using zero-sum games for LLM evaluation is not entirely new; prior work has explored game-based benchmarks. However, ZeroSumEval distinguishes itself by offering a more comprehensive, standardized, and extensible framework. The emphasis on dynamic benchmarks that resist saturation is a key contribution. The implementation details, particularly the scalable verification method for automatically validating model-generated challenges (MathQuiz, PyJail) is a notable innovation.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM evaluation: the limitations of static benchmarks. ZeroSumEval's dynamic approach provides a potentially more robust and realistic assessment of model capabilities. It offers a way to evaluate LLMs in scenarios that are less prone to data contamination and overfitting.  The framework's extensibility allows for the easy incorporation of new games and evaluation tasks, keeping the benchmark relevant as LLMs evolve. By making the code open source, the paper facilitates further research and development in LLM evaluation. The analysis revealing that top models struggle with tasks that require creativity and novel challenge creation sheds light on areas where LLMs still need significant improvement. However, the limitation in complexity for implemented games may lead to a decrease in assessment ability as LLMs get more intelligent.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The zero-sum game approach offers inherent resistance to saturation.
    *   The framework is designed for extensibility and modularity, allowing for the inclusion of new games and evaluation metrics.
    *   The scalable verification method for automatically validating challenges is a significant technical contribution.
    *   The experiments cover a wide range of models and games, providing a comprehensive analysis.
    *   The open-source release promotes further research and development.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The complexity of some games (e.g., Chess, Poker) may be limited in the current implementation; more complex game states and rules could further challenge LLMs.
    *   The reliance on LLMs to score debates introduces potential biases. A more objective scoring mechanism might be needed.
    *   The results, while insightful, primarily demonstrate *limitations* of current LLMs; more work is needed to show how ZeroSumEval can drive *improvements* in model capabilities.
    *   Some findings, such as the mixed results with CoT prompting, require further investigation to fully understand the underlying reasons.
    *   The reliance on using GPT-4 as the standard to beat can skew rankings.

*   **Potential Influence:**  ZeroSumEval has the potential to become a widely adopted framework for LLM evaluation. Its dynamic and extensible nature makes it well-suited to keep pace with the rapid advancements in LLM technology. It could influence the development of more robust and trustworthy AI models by providing a more challenging and realistic evaluation environment. It could also inform the design of new training techniques that focus on improving creativity, strategic thinking, and problem-solving skills.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

ZeroSumEval is a valuable contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. While not entirely revolutionary in its core idea, it significantly advances the concept of game-based benchmarks by providing a more comprehensive, standardized, and extensible framework. The scalable verification method is a notable technical innovation. The paper addresses a critical need for evaluation methods that are resistant to saturation and data contamination. The open-source release will likely foster further research and adoption. However, there is room for improvement in the complexity of some games and the objectivity of the evaluation metrics. The framework's potential for driving *improvements* in LLM capabilities (beyond just revealing *limitations*) needs to be further explored. Nonetheless, the paper represents a significant step forward in LLM evaluation and warrants a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt-Driven and Training-Free Forgetting Approach and Dataset for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12574v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Yu, Mohd Yamani Inda Idris, Pei Wang
- **Abstract**: The widespread adoption of diffusion models in image generation has increased the demand for privacy-compliant unlearning. However, due to the high-dimensional nature and complex feature representations of diffusion models, achieving selective unlearning remains challenging, as existing methods struggle to remove sensitive information while preserving the consistency of non-sensitive regions. To address this, we propose an Automatic Dataset Creation Framework based on prompt-based layered editing and training-free local feature removal, constructing the ForgetMe dataset and introducing the Entangled evaluation metric. The Entangled metric quantifies unlearning effectiveness by assessing the similarity and consistency between the target and background regions and supports both paired (Entangled-D) and unpaired (Entangled-S) image data, enabling unsupervised evaluation. The ForgetMe dataset encompasses a diverse set of real and synthetic scenarios, including CUB-200-2011 (Birds), Stanford-Dogs, ImageNet, and a synthetic cat dataset. We apply LoRA fine-tuning on Stable Diffusion to achieve selective unlearning on this dataset and validate the effectiveness of both the ForgetMe dataset and the Entangled metric, establishing them as benchmarks for selective unlearning. Our work provides a scalable and adaptable solution for advancing privacy-preserving generative AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ForgetMe, a new approach and dataset for evaluating selective forgetting in large generative models.  It tackles the challenge of achieving privacy-compliant unlearning in diffusion models, where removing specific features without degrading the overall generation quality is difficult.  The core contributions are: 1) Entangled, a novel evaluation metric that combines similarity and consistency between target and background regions to quantify unlearning effectiveness. It works for paired and unpaired images 2) an automatic dataset creation framework using prompt-based layered editing and training-free local feature removal. 3) The ForgetMe dataset, comprising real and synthetic images to support comprehensive unlearning evaluation.  The paper fine-tunes Stable Diffusion with LoRA on the ForgetMe dataset to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed dataset and metric.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers moderate novelty.  The concept of selective unlearning itself is not entirely new, but the specific combination of the Entangled metric, the automatic dataset generation framework, and the resulting ForgetMe dataset is a valuable contribution. The layered editing strategy is a pragmatic way to produce training examples. The application of GPT to assist with dataset creation is interesting and efficient. A weakness is the reliance on Stable Diffusion and LoRA without comparison with alternatives for fine-tuning/unlearning. It might have been stronger to show how it stacks up against more targeted model-editing approaches for diffusion models. Also, the novelty of 'Entangled' metric depends on how extensively similar metrics have been explored.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in addressing a key challenge in privacy-preserving generative AI. The widespread use of diffusion models necessitates methods for removing sensitive information, and this paper provides a concrete dataset and evaluation metric to facilitate research in this area. A robust evaluation framework is essential for progress in this domain. The code and data release promises to spur further research by providing readily available benchmarks. While the paper benchmarks against a few existing approaches, further comparisons would strengthen the impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The automatic dataset generation framework is a valuable tool that enables the creation of diverse and controlled unlearning datasets.
    *   The Entangled metric addresses a critical gap in existing evaluation methods by considering both forgetting effectiveness and background consistency.
    *   The ForgetMe dataset is a useful resource for researchers working on selective unlearning.
    *   Clear presentation and well-defined methodology.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method may encounter limitations with transparent objects or images heavily dependent on prompt specificity.
    *   Limited experimentation on comparing the proposed methods with existing unlearning techniques and model editing for diffusion models.
    *   The novelty of the evaluation metric 'Entangled' needs more stringent justification and more detailed comparisons with other region-based approaches, if they exist.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has good potential to influence the field by providing a benchmark for selective unlearning in generative models. It can encourage researchers to develop more effective and reliable unlearning techniques.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a moderately novel and significant contribution to the field of privacy-preserving generative AI. The proposed dataset and evaluation metric address a crucial challenge in selective unlearning for diffusion models. While the approach's novelty is somewhat limited by its reliance on existing techniques, the specific combination of elements and the resulting practical toolkit (dataset + metric) warrants a reasonable score. The limitations related to prompt sensitivity, the absence of comparison to similar metrics, and handling transparent objects somewhat restrain the paper's overall impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Identifying and Mitigating the Influence of the Prior Distribution in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12585v1)
- **Authors**: Liyi Zhang, Veniamin Veselovsky, R. Thomas McCoy, Thomas L. Griffiths
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) sometimes fail to respond appropriately to deterministic tasks -- such as counting or forming acronyms -- because the implicit prior distribution they have learned over sequences of tokens influences their responses. In this work, we show that, in at least some cases, LLMs actually compute the information needed to perform these tasks correctly, and we identify some interventions that can allow them to access this information to improve their performance. First, we show that simply prompting the language model to not rely on its prior knowledge leads to dramatic improvements in prior-dominated tasks. We then use mechanistic interpretability techniques to localize the prior within the LLM and manipulate the extent to which that prior influences its responses. Specifically, we show that it is possible to identify layers of the underlying neural network that correlate with the prior probability of a response and that lightweight finetuning of these layers with basic prompts on prior-dominated tasks achieves high performance on held-out answers. These results suggest that the information required to produce a correct response is contained within the representations of the problems formed by the models. Furthermore, we show that this finetuning is significantly more effective for prior-dominated tasks, and that the error after finetuning is no longer correlated with the prior. Our results suggest that it may be possible to define effective methods for manipulating the extent to which LLMs rely upon their priors in solving problems, potentially increasing their performance in settings where LLMs hallucinate for reasons related to the prior probability of token sequences.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how the prior distribution learned by Large Language Models (LLMs) influences their performance on deterministic tasks where they should ideally rely solely on the provided input. The authors demonstrate that LLMs sometimes fail in these tasks because they are overly influenced by their prior knowledge about token sequences. The study explores methods to mitigate this influence, including prompting strategies and targeted fine-tuning. They find that specifically instructing the model to ignore its prior knowledge can improve performance, and that fine-tuning specific layers of the network on tasks dominated by prior biases leads to significant improvements. Mechanistic interpretability techniques, specifically logit lens, are used to identify the layers most strongly correlated with the prior distribution.  The paper suggests that the information required to solve the tasks is present within the LLM's representations, but the prior distribution distracts the model from accessing it.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a valuable exploration into how prior distributions affect LLM behavior on deterministic tasks. While the issue of LLM reliance on priors has been explored before (McCoy et al., 2023), this paper takes a significant step further. It provides evidence for *where* this prior is encoded in the model, rather than simply demonstrating its existence. The use of mechanistic interpretability to identify relevant layers is a notable contribution. The targeted fine-tuning strategy is also a novel approach to mitigate the effects of the prior.
*   **Significance:** The findings have practical implications. Demonstrating the ability to identify and mitigate prior influences is important for improving the reliability of LLMs in scenarios where accuracy is paramount. The methods proposed could lead to more robust and less "hallucination"-prone LLMs. The work also provides a valuable framework for understanding and controlling LLM behavior more generally. The results are especially relevant to tasks where LLMs are deployed in settings where the correct answer is not necessarily the most probable from the models training data.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of prior influence in deterministic tasks.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors use a combination of quantitative evaluation, mechanistic interpretability, and targeted interventions, providing a comprehensive analysis.
    *   **Practical Solutions:** The prompting and fine-tuning strategies offer practical ways to improve LLM performance.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper doesn't solely rely on observed accuracy, but also uses logit lens to analyze how prior beliefs are encoded in different model layers.
    *   **Stratified Finetuning:** The usage of stratified finetuning is a strong point in preventing the LLM from memorization during finetuning.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on a specific set of tasks (counting, shift cipher, acronyms). While these are good examples, the generalizability of the findings to other tasks and domains needs further investigation.
    *   **Model Specificity:** The experiments are conducted using LLAMA 3 with 8B parameters. It is necessary to show that these techniques also work across different LLMs and LLM sizes.
    *   **Reliance on "Logit Lens":** The logit lens is a method for mechanistic interpretability that is not without its criticisms in the literature. Although widely used, caution should still be taken when interpreting the results of experiments based on logit lens.
    *   **Lack of explanation of the unsuccesful linear intervention methods:** More detail should be given about the unsuccesful linear intervention methods in Appendix A.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the design of LLM training and fine-tuning strategies, as well as the development of methods for controlling LLM behavior.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7 to this paper. The research addresses an important problem, offers clear evidence of prior influence, and proposes effective methods for mitigation. The use of mechanistic interpretability is a significant strength. However, the limitations regarding task scope, model specificity, and some methodological points prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The limited discussion of unsuccessful attempts is also a minor weakness. Nevertheless, the paper represents a valuable advancement in our understanding of LLM behavior and offers promising directions for future research.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Simplifying Graph Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12588v1)
- **Authors**: Liheng Ma, Soumyasundar Pal, Yingxue Zhang, Philip H. S. Torr, Mark Coates
- **Abstract**: Transformers have attained outstanding performance across various modalities, employing scaled-dot-product (SDP) attention mechanisms. Researchers have attempted to migrate Transformers to graph learning, but most advanced Graph Transformers are designed with major architectural differences, either integrating message-passing or incorporating sophisticated attention mechanisms. These complexities prevent the easy adoption of Transformer training advances. We propose three simple modifications to the plain Transformer to render it applicable to graphs without introducing major architectural distortions. Specifically, we advocate for the use of (1) simplified $L_2$ attention to measure the magnitude closeness of tokens; (2) adaptive root-mean-square normalization to preserve token magnitude information; and (3) a relative positional encoding bias with a shared encoder. Significant performance gains across a variety of graph datasets justify the effectiveness of our proposed modifications. Furthermore, empirical evaluation on the expressiveness benchmark reveals noteworthy realized expressiveness in the graph isomorphism.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the increasing complexity of Graph Transformer architectures, arguing that many current approaches deviate significantly from the original "plain" Transformer design, hindering the transfer of advancements from other domains and potentially obstructing multi-modal unification. The authors propose three simple modifications to the plain Transformer to make it more suitable for graph learning: (1) simplified L2 attention, measuring magnitude closeness; (2) adaptive root-mean-square normalization (AdaRMSN) to preserve magnitude information; and (3) a relative positional encoding bias with a shared encoder.  They demonstrate empirical improvements on graph classification and regression tasks, and improved empirical expressiveness on a graph isomorphism benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's core novelty lies in its *simplification* of existing Graph Transformers, rather than introducing a completely novel architecture. The modifications, while individually not entirely new (e.g., adaptive normalization is inspired by conditional normalization), are combined strategically and presented as a means to retain the core Transformer structure while addressing the specific challenges of graph data. The simplification aspect is important because the authors are advocating a return to basics. The L2 attention, while not conceptually groundbreaking, presents a straightforward and computationally efficient alternative to more complex attention mechanisms in graph Transformers. The use of sinusoidal positional encoding to improve expressivity is inspired by the work of NeRF and is a good analogy to consider.

* **Significance:** The significance is more moderate than transformative. The empirical results are promising, demonstrating performance improvements across several benchmark datasets. The performance comparison to GraphGPS and GRIT is particularly valuable, given their widespread adoption. The improvement on the empirical expressiveness benchmark (BREC) is important because expressivity is a fundamental property of graph neural networks, which dictates its ability to distinguish graph structures. The value of this work is to enable others in the field to more easily apply advancements and knowledge from the broader Transformer literature to the graph domain. The paper is also more generalizable to multiple modalities.

* **Strengths:**

    * **Clear Motivation:** The paper effectively articulates the problem of increasing complexity in Graph Transformers and the potential drawbacks of this trend.
    * **Simplicity and Generalizability:** The proposed modifications are simple to implement and integrate into existing Transformer architectures, which promotes broader adoption.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  The authors provide strong empirical evidence to support their claims, including performance comparisons on benchmark datasets and expressivity analysis. The ablations are useful to confirm design choices.
    * **Well written and clearly presented:** The authors provide a clear overview of the field.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the empirical results are compelling, the paper lacks a more in-depth theoretical analysis of the proposed modifications.  For instance, a formal analysis of the expressivity gains from the sinusoidal encoding would be valuable. Furthermore, a theoretical justification for simplified L2 attention could strengthen the argument.  The paper mentions but does not deeply explore theoretical expressivity, which is disappointing.
    * **Incremental Innovation:** The proposed methods are best characterized as incremental improvements rather than radical innovations. They are more of re-designs than a completely novel design.
    * **Parameter Budget:** The authors should include a parameter budget for the models they compare against, making the comparison fairer.
    * **Limited Scope:**  While the modifications are presented as general, the experiments focus primarily on graph classification and regression tasks. The applicability to other graph learning tasks (e.g., node classification, link prediction) could be further explored.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future Graph Transformers by encouraging a focus on simplicity and generalizability. It also provides a clear direction for researchers to explore the transfer of advancements from other Transformer domains to graph learning. Given the simplicity of the modifications, this paper has the potential to have great impact.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign this paper a score of **7**. The paper presents valuable improvements to existing Graph Transformers by emphasizing simplicity and generalizability.  The empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed modifications, and the analysis of empirical expressivity provides insights into the model's capabilities. However, the lack of deeper theoretical analysis and the incremental nature of the innovation slightly limit its overall impact. The potential for wider adoption due to the simplicity of the changes and the focus on unification across modalities elevates the paper above a purely incremental contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GeoSense: Evaluating Identification and Application of Geometric Principles in Multimodal Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12597v1)
- **Authors**: Liangyu Xu, Yingxiu Zhao, Jingyun Wang, Yingyao Wang, Bu Pi, Chen Wang, Mingliang Zhang, Jihao Gu, Xiang Li, Xiaoyong Zhu, Jun Song, Bo Zheng
- **Abstract**: Geometry problem-solving (GPS), a challenging task requiring both visual comprehension and symbolic reasoning, effectively measures the reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Humans exhibit strong reasoning ability in this task through accurate identification and adaptive application of geometric principles within visual contexts. However, existing benchmarks fail to jointly assess both dimensions of the human-like geometric reasoning mechanism in MLLMs, remaining a critical gap in assessing their ability to tackle GPS. To this end, we introduce GeoSense, the first comprehensive bilingual benchmark designed to systematically evaluate the geometric reasoning abilities of MLLMs through the lens of geometric principles. GeoSense features a five-level hierarchical framework of geometric principles spanning plane and solid geometry, an intricately annotated dataset of 1,789 problems, and an innovative evaluation strategy. Through extensive experiments on GeoSense with various open-source and closed-source MLLMs, we observe that Gemini-2.0-pro-flash performs best, achieving an overall score of $65.3$. Our in-depth analysis reveals that the identification and application of geometric principles remain a bottleneck for leading MLLMs, jointly hindering their reasoning abilities. These findings underscore GeoSense's potential to guide future advancements in MLLMs' geometric reasoning capabilities, paving the way for more robust and human-like reasoning in artificial intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GeoSense paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GeoSense, a new bilingual benchmark designed to evaluate the geometric reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs).  GeoSense focuses on assessing two key aspects of geometric reasoning: the ability to accurately *identify* relevant geometric principles and the ability to *apply* those principles correctly within a visual context. The benchmark comprises a hierarchical framework of 148 geometric principles, a dataset of 1,789 geometry problems, and an evaluation strategy using two key metrics: Geometry Principle Identification (GPI) and Geometry Principle Application (GPA), as well as overall answer accuracy (ACC). Experiments conducted with various MLLMs reveal that existing models, while strong in computation, struggle with understanding and applying geometric principles effectively. The paper highlights Gemini-2.0-pro-flash as the best performing model, but also demonstrates the need for improvements in applying geometric principles in visual scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a substantial contribution by focusing on geometric reasoning from the *perspective of geometric principles*.  While existing geometry benchmarks exist, GeoSense's focus on assessing principle identification and application is a significant departure.  Previous benchmarks primarily evaluate final answer accuracy or, at best, reasoning steps without explicitly evaluating the understanding and application of underpinning geometric principles. This explicit emphasis on geometric principles is novel and adds a layer of interpretability to MLLM evaluation. The bilingual aspect, while not groundbreaking on its own, increases the accessibility and potential impact of the benchmark. The hierarchical framework also adds depth to the evaluation process.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they pinpoint a specific weakness in current MLLMs: the difficulty in accurately identifying and adaptively applying geometric principles. This is crucial because it suggests that simply scaling up models or improving visual perception alone will not necessarily solve the geometric reasoning problem. Addressing this specific bottleneck – the "geometric principle gap" - is essential for achieving more human-like reasoning in AI. The benchmark allows for more fine-grained analysis, revealing that even models with good overall accuracy may still struggle with the adaptive application of principles. This fine-grained analysis can help in creating improved MLLMs. The comprehensive framework also bridges the gap between human geometric intuition and MLLM performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Focus:**  The paper has a well-defined focus on geometric principles, making its contribution clear and impactful.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The hierarchical framework and the GPI/GPA metrics provide a thorough evaluation of MLLM geometric reasoning abilities.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper includes a detailed error analysis, providing valuable insights into the specific weaknesses of different MLLMs.
    *   **Bilingual:** The availability of the benchmark in both English and Chinese expands its accessibility and potential impact.
    *   **Open Source Availability**: The data being released is critical for the community to study and build from.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **GPT-4 dependency in annotation and evaluation:** The reliance on GPT-4 for annotation and evaluation raises concerns about potential biases or limitations inherent in the GPT-4 model.  While human review is included, the initial prompting and principle extraction by GPT-4 may influence the annotation process.
    *   **Complexity Metric:** The use of number of geometric principles as a proxy for problem complexity is simplistic. There could be cases with fewer geometric principles but requiring a very sophisticated application of those to arrive at the answer.

*   **Potential Influence:** GeoSense has the potential to become a widely used benchmark in the field of multimodal reasoning.  By highlighting the importance of geometric principles, it can encourage researchers to develop MLLMs that are better at understanding and applying these principles. The fine-grained analysis offered by GeoSense can also help researchers to identify specific areas where MLLMs need improvement. The benchmark could also drive development of new architectures or training strategies that explicitly address the geometric principle gap.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper is valuable, the reliance on GPT-4 raises significant concerns that prevent the paper from being assigned a score in the high range. GeoSense's focus on geometric principles is indeed novel, and the benchmark provides a more nuanced evaluation of MLLMs in this domain. It has the potential to influence future research directions. However, The complexity metrics should also be better defined.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Code Copycat Conundrum: Demystifying Repetition in LLM-based Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12608v1)
- **Authors**: Mingwei Liu, Juntao Li, Ying Wang, Xueying Du, Zuoyu Ou, Qiuyuan Chen, Bingxu An, Zhao Wei, Yong Xu, Fangming Zou, Xin Peng, Yiling Lou
- **Abstract**: Despite recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) for code generation, the quality of LLM-generated code still faces significant challenges. One significant issue is code repetition, which refers to the model's tendency to generate structurally redundant code, resulting in inefficiencies and reduced readability. To address this, we conduct the first empirical study to investigate the prevalence and nature of repetition across 19 state-of-the-art code LLMs using three widely-used benchmarks. Our study includes both quantitative and qualitative analyses, revealing that repetition is pervasive and manifests at various granularities and extents, including character, statement, and block levels. We further summarize a taxonomy of 20 repetition patterns. Building on our findings, we propose DeRep, a rule-based technique designed to detect and mitigate repetition in generated code. We evaluate DeRep using both open-source benchmarks and in an industrial setting. Our results demonstrate that DeRep significantly outperforms baselines in reducing repetition (with an average improvements of 91.3%, 93.5%, and 79.9% in rep-3, rep-line, and sim-line metrics) and enhancing code quality (with a Pass@1 increase of 208.3% over greedy search). Furthermore, integrating DeRep improves the performance of existing repetition mitigation methods, with Pass@1 improvements ranging from 53.7% to 215.7%.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Code Copycat Conundrum: Demystifying Repetition in LLM-based Code Generation."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the problem of code repetition in code generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors perform an empirical study on 19 state-of-the-art code LLMs using three widely-used benchmarks, and qualitatively derive a taxonomy of 20 repetition patterns.  They find that repetition is pervasive across different granularities (character, statement, and block levels).  Based on their findings, they propose a rule-based repetition mitigation technique called DeRep, which detects and fixes repetition issues.  They evaluate DeRep on open-source benchmarks and in an industrial setting, showing that DeRep significantly reduces repetition and enhances code quality, outperforming existing baselines and improving the performance of other repetition mitigation methods when integrated.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes several contributions, but the level of novelty is somewhat mixed.
    *   The empirical study is the **first systematic study** of repetition issues in LLM-based *code* generation. While repetition has been studied in general text generation, the specific characteristics and mitigation strategies required for code are distinct. This is a key point of novelty.
    *   The taxonomy of 20 repetition patterns is a valuable contribution, as it provides a structured understanding of the different ways repetition manifests in code. This is a reasonably novel and useful contribution.
    *   DeRep, the proposed rule-based technique, is more incremental. Rule-based approaches for code analysis and modification are common. The novelty lies in the *specific* rules designed based on the empirical findings of *this* study. This aspect of DeRep’s design adds a level of specificity that isn’t widely available. The authors need to emphasize more clearly the unique rule formulations that distinguish DeRep from existing similar techniques.
    *   The evaluation across open-source and industrial settings strengthens the practical relevance and applicability of the work.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The problem of code repetition is a significant one. It directly impacts code quality, readability, maintainability, and efficiency. By highlighting the pervasiveness of this problem in LLM-generated code, the paper raises an important concern for the wider community.
    *   The DeRep technique offers a practical solution that demonstrably improves code quality. The performance gains are significant, suggesting that this is a valuable tool for improving LLM-based code generation.
    *   The industrial setting evaluation adds weight to the practical significance of the research, demonstrating its applicability in real-world scenarios.
    *   However, while significant, the proposed approach is relatively narrow. While the improvements are considerable, the nature of the solution—rule-based—suggests it might not generalize well to unseen repetition patterns beyond the ones identified in the study. Future work should explore if it is possible to create more generalizable models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong empirical grounding:** The study is well-grounded in empirical evidence, with a comprehensive evaluation across a range of models and datasets.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** Addresses a real-world problem in code generation.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology is clearly explained and well-executed.
    *   **Demonstrated improvements:** The evaluation convincingly shows the effectiveness of DeRep.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Technique:** The DeRep technique, while effective, is somewhat incremental. The novelty is limited to specific rules derived from the study’s findings.
    *   **Generality:** The rule-based nature of DeRep might limit its ability to generalize to new, unseen repetition patterns.
    *   **Over-reliance on benchmarks:** There is a need to explore if DeRep still exhibits similar performance on code generated in real-world projects.
    * The paper could benefit from discussing limitations more explicitly, particularly regarding the generalizability of DeRep and the potential need for further research into more adaptable solutions.

*   **Potential influence:** The paper is likely to influence research in code generation by highlighting the problem of repetition and offering a practical mitigation strategy. The taxonomy of repetition patterns will also be valuable for researchers working on this problem. It may also encourage the development of more sophisticated repetition mitigation techniques based on machine learning.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the above analysis, I assign the paper a score of **7**.

*   The empirical study and taxonomy provide a novel and significant contribution by systematically characterizing code repetition in LLM generated code, where the paper is the first to do this systematically.
*   The DeRep technique, while effective, is somewhat incremental, which limits its overall novelty.
*   The evaluation is strong, but the limitations of the rule-based approach and the potential for improved generalization need to be acknowledged more explicitly.

Therefore, a score of 7 reflects the paper's valuable contributions to the field while acknowledging its limitations and potential for further improvement.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Packing Input Frame Context in Next-Frame Prediction Models for Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12626v1)
- **Authors**: Lvmin Zhang, Maneesh Agrawala
- **Abstract**: We present a neural network structure, FramePack, to train next-frame (or next-frame-section) prediction models for video generation. The FramePack compresses input frames to make the transformer context length a fixed number regardless of the video length. As a result, we are able to process a large number of frames using video diffusion with computation bottleneck similar to image diffusion. This also makes the training video batch sizes significantly higher (batch sizes become comparable to image diffusion training). We also propose an anti-drifting sampling method that generates frames in inverted temporal order with early-established endpoints to avoid exposure bias (error accumulation over iterations). Finally, we show that existing video diffusion models can be finetuned with FramePack, and their visual quality may be improved because the next-frame prediction supports more balanced diffusion schedulers with less extreme flow shift timesteps.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FramePack, a novel neural network structure designed to improve next-frame prediction models for video generation. FramePack addresses the "forgetting-drifting dilemma" by progressively compressing input frames based on their estimated importance, ensuring a fixed total context length for the transformer regardless of video duration. This compression is achieved by manipulating the kernel size of transformer patchify layers. The authors also propose anti-drifting sampling methods, including generating endpoint frames first and inverted temporal sampling, to incorporate bi-directional context and mitigate error accumulation. They demonstrate FramePack's effectiveness by fine-tuning existing video diffusion models and show improved visual quality and model responsiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a core problem:** The forgetting-drifting dilemma is a significant challenge in video generation, and the paper tackles it directly.
    *   **Novel technique:** FramePack provides a relatively simple yet effective way to manage context length and prioritize relevant frames. The dynamic compression based on temporal proximity is a sound strategy.
    *   **Practical applicability:** The method can be applied to existing video diffusion models, suggesting it has broad utility. The reported batch size improvements are also notable for practical training.
    *   **Good Ablation Study**: Ablation studies are performed that show compelling evidence for the contribution of the components.
    *   **Strong evaluation:** The paper uses a comprehensive set of metrics (including human evaluation and a novel "drifting" metric) to thoroughly evaluate the proposed method.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental novelty:** While FramePack is a good idea and seems to work well, the individual components (attention mechanisms, progressive compression, etc.) are not entirely new. The innovation lies in the specific combination and application to the video generation problem.
    *   **Simplicity Assumptions**: The assumption of temporal proximity equalling importance is simple and might not be the optimal choice.
    *   **Ablation Studies**: While informative, the parameters within the ablations could use some more investigation, the kernel sizes, training parameters, could all be optimized more fully for each variation.
    *   **Limited scope**: The experiments focus primarily on diffusion models. FramePack might not be as effective for other types of video generation architectures.
    *   **No concrete theoretical foundation for anti-drifting methods**: The improvement shown by introducing the endpoint context is empirical, and lacks a theoretical foundation.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Improved efficiency:** The ability to process longer videos with similar computational costs as image diffusion is significant, potentially opening up new avenues for high-resolution long-form video generation.
    *   **Enhanced Visual Quality:** The results suggest that the method can improve visual quality, model responsiveness, and higher batch sizes in training.
    *   **Impact:** The research contributes to a better understanding of how context management and sampling strategies affect video generation quality. Other researchers can build upon FramePack or use it as a benchmark for future techniques.

**Novelty and Significance Score:**

The FramePack architecture is an interesting idea, and the results presented are compelling. However, it builds on existing concepts like attention mechanisms and progressive compression, resulting in only incremental novelty. The anti-drifting method provides a very interesting empirical observation that can prompt further study. While useful and impactful, it isn't groundbreaking.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's strengths in addressing a relevant problem, proposing a practically useful method, and conducting a comprehensive evaluation. However, the limited novelty of the individual components and its scope of improvement reduces the rating below 8. While FramePack contributes to advancements in video generation, a higher score would require more substantial innovation or a more significant theoretical breakthrough. The impact is evident, but its transformative potential is moderate rather than exceptional.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Characterizing Subjectivity of Individuals through Modeling Value Conflicts and Trade-offs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12633v1)
- **Authors**: Younghun Lee, Dan Goldwasser
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) not only have solved complex reasoning problems but also exhibit remarkable performance in tasks that require subjective decision making. Existing studies suggest that LLM generations can be subjectively grounded to some extent, yet exploring whether LLMs can account for individual-level subjectivity has not been sufficiently studied. In this paper, we characterize subjectivity of individuals on social media and infer their moral judgments using LLMs. We propose a framework, SOLAR (Subjective Ground with Value Abstraction), that observes value conflicts and trade-offs in the user-generated texts to better represent subjective ground of individuals. Empirical results show that our framework improves overall inference results as well as performance on controversial situations. Additionally, we qualitatively show that SOLAR provides explanations about individuals' value preferences, which can further account for their judgments.
